\small
\paragraph{How to read this map.} This companion to the survey lists every paper of the consolidated tree{,} 2{,}456 papers in 75 nodes over seven clusters. Each paper has one home node and is cited there once. Other placements are recorded in \texttt{tree\_v3.json} as cross-links. This v3 map merges small and adjacent v2 nodes; each merged node lists the v2 nodes it absorbed, and every paper keeps its v2 node as a subtopic tag. Near-duplicate support and background papers are grouped into named families of at least four papers that share one core idea; within a node each family is one line, and Appendix~\ref{app:families} indexes all families. A node gives its relation to the root, its idea, its core papers with the reason each is core, its support papers, its families, its remaining support and background papers and the lineage edges that touch it. A dagger \dag{} marks a paper that has not been read, neither in full nor from an abstract. Appendix~\ref{app:index} indexes every key by node.

\paragraph{Relation codes.} Each code states how the node's objective relates to the root, $\min_\pi H_\mu[d^\pi K]$: $=$ same objective; $\subset$ special case of ours; $\supset$ generalisation of ours; $-$ opposite sign; $\approx$ same mechanism, different objective; $\sim$ same sign, other quantity; \textsf{T} tool we use; \textsf{L} precondition or limitation; \textsf{C} contrast. In lineage lists, $\to$ reads older to newer, $\ast$ marks an inferred edge, and $\times$ marks an edge whose other end lives in another cluster.

\section{S1 Foundations, the good regulator and control of outcome distributions}
384 papers in 10 nodes: 18 core, 135 support, 231 background; 10 families hold 147 papers.

\medskip\textsc{I. Origins: regulation as reducing outcome variety}

\subsection{S1.01 cybernetic-origins}\label{node:S1-S1-01-cybernetic-origins}
\textit{Merged from} S1.03-perceptual-control (20). \textbf{Relation} $=$: Same objective, informal: "small outcome variety" is the criterion that Conant and Ashby later wrote as H(Z). [S1.03-perceptual-control] Same objective, where Z is the controlled perception. Evidence:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Gulrez 2021 plays Atari with no training.
\item Catenacci Volpi 2023 finds skilled balance has low H(S) and high H(A).
\item Powers 1978, eq. 8: q\_i = q\_i* + h(q\_d)/(1 \ensuremath{-} UV), so an error-controlled residual vanishes only as loop gain grows without bound. This is a quantitative form of Conant's limit.
\end{itemize}


\textbf{Idea.} Regulation keeps essential variables within limits against disturbance: Ashby's D\ensuremath{\to}R\ensuremath{\to}E variety framing, Sommerhoff's setup, Craik's internal models, Wiener's cybernetics and Schr\"odinger's organisms exporting entropy.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S1.03-perceptual-control [=]: Powers: behaviour is the control of perception.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Ashby-introduction56}: Founding: regulation as blocking variety; Shannon form H(E) \textgreater{}= H(D) + H\_D(R) - H(R) is the budget on our objective
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S1.02 variety-and-information-accounting}\label{node:S1-S1-02-variety-and-information-accounting}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation: the lower bound, or budget, on our objective. Error-controlled versus cause-controlled is our reward-channel versus state-observation distinction. Musgrave 1989 is \textbf{same sign, other quantity}: it drives H(A \textbar{} S = cell) \ensuremath{\to} 0, a deterministic policy, with no outcome entropy.

\textbf{Idea.} Information accounting of regulation, in five strands:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Ashby's law H(E) \ensuremath{\geq} H(D) + H\_D(R) \ensuremath{-} H(R), with multi-scale and thermodynamic versions by Siegenfeld and Boyd.
\item Conant 1969. Cause-controlled regulation can reach H(Z) = 0, error-controlled cannot. The regulation gain against the constant-action baseline H\_o(Z) is V = I(R;S) \ensuremath{-} I(R;Z) + [H\_\{R,Z\}(S) \ensuremath{-} H\_\{r\_o,Z\}(S)], so V \ensuremath{\leq} 0 when I(R;S) = 0, and error control gives V = red(S) \ensuremath{-} red(Z). The paper also treats path regulation through the minimum entropy rate H\^{}L(Z).
\item Conant 1976, the partition law: \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_j H(X\_j) = throughput + blockage + coordination + noise, with regulation as blockage, minimising the output information T(E:S\_o) at fixed H(E).
\item Cost read as entropy. Saridis: the max-entropy law over controls p(u) \ensuremath{\propto} exp(\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\mu}V) makes min V equivalent to min H(u). Engell: requisite variety restated through rate distortion for any expected cost, with cause control reaching \ensuremath{\delta} = 0.
\item Weidemann--Jones, the continuous-time Gaussian strand. Shannon entropy there collapses to log-variance, so Jones uses the Kullback entropy rate, with H\_\~Y[Y] + I[E;Y] = H\_\~Y[Y\textbar{}E].
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Conant-information69}: Founding: error- vs cause-controlled regulation; V=I(R;S)-I(R;Z)+[...] (eq 1), V\textless{}=0 when I(R;S)=0; path regulation and the constant-action baseline H\_o(Z)
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{II. The good regulator theorem and its repairs}

\subsection{S1.04 good-regulator-theorem}\label{node:S1-S1-04-good-regulator-theorem}
\textit{Merged from} S1.05-good-regulator-under-partial-observation (3); S1.06-set-valued-algorithmic-semantic-regulators (10); S1.12-closed-loop-entropy-bounds (4). \textbf{Relation} $\approx$/$\subset$/$=$: Same mechanism, single step. Concavity of entropy gives a deterministic optimum; we lift it to occupancies. Lawrence's \texttt{thm:goodreg} and \texttt{thm:simplest-regulator} restate it, and \texttt{prop:stochastic-deterministic} already uses the polytope argument for a stochastic channel. [S1.05-good-regulator-under-partial-observation] Special case: the single-decision case of our belief-MDP remark. [S1.06-set-valued-algorithmic-semantic-regulators] Same mechanism, different objective. The support of Z must lie inside G, the viability or satisficing twin of min H(Z). Here the model is an interpretation and may be trivial. [S1.12-closed-loop-entropy-bounds] Same objective and same mechanism, single step, state equal to outcome. The three terms map onto our budget H(Z) = I(S;Z) + I(A;Z\textbar{}S) + H(Z\textbar{}S,A).

\textbf{Idea.} Conant--Ashby 1970. At an H(Z) optimum, every action used at s gives the same outcome, so the simplest optimal regulator is a map h: S \ensuremath{\to} R. The commentaries of Baez, Moss and Harwood agree that this is function-hood only, and h may be constant.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S1.05-good-regulator-under-partial-observation [\ensuremath{\subset}]: When the regulator sees only X, the simplest optimum is a deterministic function of the posterior P(S\textbar{}X).
\item Absorbed S1.06-set-valued-algorithmic-semantic-regulators [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Restatements with a different success criterion:
\item Possibilistic: a forward-closed regulating set R \ensuremath{\subseteq} G exists iff there is a subjective belief map, Virgo 2025.
\item Absorbed S1.12-closed-loop-entropy-bounds [=]: Touchette--Lloyd:
\item Open-loop pure control is optimal, by concavity of entropy, Theorem 1.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Conant-good70}: Founding: min H(Z) gives a deterministic simplest regulator h:S-\textgreater{}R; the single-step mechanism we lift to occupancies
\item \citet{Wentworth-fixing21}: Posterior form of the theorem and the gooder regulator; leaves the entropy-criterion isomorphism open; also in B9
\item \citet{Harwood-generalization25}: Nearest prior proof: entropy version with imperfect information via concavity of mixtures, single decision; also in B9
\item \citet{Virgo-good25}: Set-valued twin: forward-closed regulating set iff subjective good regulator; viability version of our objective; also in B9
\item \citet{Touchette-information00}: Direct precedent: Thm 1 open-loop pure control optimal by concavity of entropy; Eq. 3 closed-loop gain \textless{}= I(X;C); also in B6
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{III. When is a real model forced?}

\subsection{S1.07 internal-model-principle}\label{node:S1-S1-07-internal-model-principle}
\textit{Merged from} S1.08-abstract-and-bayesian-IMP (9); S1.09-internal-models-in-biology-and-motor-control (17); S1.10-world-model-necessity (16). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation. Structurally stable exactness, not optimality, turns "function" into "faithful model". This is the dark-room and persistent-disturbance resolution of the root. Sepulchre's contrast says a coarser regulated variable Z weakens the model requirement; in our terms that is the choice of K. [S1.08-abstract-and-bayesian-IMP] Precondition or limitation. It names exactly which assumption turns a lossy model into a faithful one: Baltieri's Theorem II.13 versus II.15, which matches Lawrence's lossy versus faithful. It bridges the IMP, the good regulator and filtering. Wonham 1976 already cites Conant--Ashby. [S1.09-internal-models-in-biology-and-motor-control] Precondition or limitation, empirical. Exact homeostasis of an outcome forces a disturbance model. The forward model matches Lawrence's \texttt{eq:fwd-loss}. The McNamee inverse model p\_inv(u \textbar{} g) is a goal-conditioned control posterior, \emph{not} \texttt{eq:inv-loss}, which recovers a\_t from z\_t and z\_\{t+1\}. [S1.10-world-model-necessity] Precondition or limitation. What forces the model is task richness, whereas our objective is one task. Richens's Theorem 2 on myopic agents mirrors Conant 1969. Baltieri's three objects match Lawrence's policy, consequence and interaction models in \texttt{tab:taxonomy}.

\textbf{Idea.} Francis--Wonham and Davison. Exact asymptotic rejection of an exosystem signal with \ensuremath{\sigma}(A\_2) in the closed right half-plane, by error feedback, \emph{that survives small parameter perturbations}, forces the controller to contain a copy of the exosystem dynamics, Francis 1976 Thms 2--4. Persistence alone does not force it. Davison gives the existence condition rank[A \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{\lambda}I, B; C, D] = n + r at the exosystem roots. Hepburn--Wonham and Isidori--Byrnes carry this to nonlinear output regulation, with the regulator equations as the solvability condition.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Learning strand. Wang 2025 learns a generic internal model with a polynomial steady-state generator, without optimality. Adib Yaghmaie 2019 is average-cost RL output regulation with the exosignal v \emph{measured and fed forward}, u = u(x, v), so no internal model is learned.
\item Sepulchre 2025: regulating discrete events needs no exact internal generator, while regulating trajectories does.
\item Absorbed S1.08-abstract-and-bayesian-IMP [L]: Structure-free IMPs:
\item set-theoretic, first in Wonham 1976 on abstract automata: under detectability and a feedback condition, a unique injective map \=a with \=a\ensuremath{\circ}\ensuremath{\gamma}\textsuperscript{+} = \ensuremath{\gamma}\textsuperscript{+}\ensuremath{\circ}\ensuremath{\alpha}\textsuperscript{+} exists, a faithful dynamic model; Wonham 2018 restates it;
\item nonlinear, where adaptation plus signal detection implies an internal model, Sontag 2003; Andrews 2006 gives the approximate linear version, limsup\textbar{}y\textbar{} \ensuremath{\leq} O(\ensuremath{\varepsilon}) implies an internal model that becomes exact as \ensuremath{\varepsilon} \ensuremath{\to} 0;
\item category-theoretic, where a controller is a model and has a possibilistic filtering reading, Baltieri 2025, with his coalgebraic argument against the brain-as-copy reading of predictive processing;
\item bisimulation for robots, Weinstein 2024.
\item Absorbed S1.09-internal-models-in-biology-and-motor-control [L]: - Robust perfect adaptation needs integral feedback or an internal model.
\item Absorbed S1.10-world-model-necessity [L]: Must a competent agent have a world model?
\item Volkinshtein 2011, Thm 9: a forward model is forced only when the task set has no NSCTP state pairs; a binary task set needs none.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Francis-internal76}: Founding IMP: structurally stable exact rejection of an exosystem with sigma(A2) in the closed RHP forces a copy, Thms 2-4; persistence alone does not; cites Conant-Ashby in Sec 2
\item \citet{Richens-general25}: Main modern competitor claim: world models from goal-conditioned competence; dismisses Conant-Ashby as determinism only, which is our mechanism
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S1.11 identifiability-from-behaviour}\label{node:S1-S1-11-identifiability-from-behaviour}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: Tool we use: it quantifies how informative our deterministic optimum is, and Letcher's algebra is our occupancy algebra. Bellot is a limitation, since positivity excludes the deterministic optimum. Heufer is opposite in shape: it tests mixture-loving preferences, whereas \ensuremath{-}H seeks vertices and has many satiation points.

\textbf{Idea.} What behaviour reveals about the environment:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Letcher: indicator-goal Q-values are occupancies, so the kernel follows by inversion.
\item Batra: optimal actions fix the kernel only up to an n(n\ensuremath{-}1)-dimensional family.
\item Harwood--Faustino--Altair: a deterministic optimum carries n log m bits on average over kernels.
\item Bellot 2025 bounds the out-of-domain decisions of a grounded agent, Thm 1, under positivity P\_d(v) \textgreater{} 0.
\item Heufer 2013 tests quasiconcave preferences on the probability simplex from choices on affine budgets, using a known single satiation point.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Geadah-inferring24}: routed by merge from v1 node 'identifiability: what optimal regulation reveals'; routed by orchestrator from B16/identifiability/kernel-from-behaviour
\item \citet{Yu-joint26}: routed by merge from v1 node 'identifiability: what optimal regulation reveals'; routed by orchestrator from B16/identifiability/kernel-from-behaviour
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{IV. Information limits of control}

\subsection{S1.13 entropy-rate-and-data-rate-limits}\label{node:S1-S1-13-entropy-rate-and-data-rate-limits}
\textit{Merged from} S1.14-thermodynamic-and-biological-costs (16). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation. These bound the entropy \emph{rate}. Because the marginal entropy of a stationary process is at least its entropy rate, Zhang's Thm 1 is also a floor on our marginal H(Z) for LTI loops: spectral redistribution can lower H(Z) only down to that rate, by whitening. Tanaka's randomised optimum is the convex contrast to our vertex. [S1.14-thermodynamic-and-biological-costs] Precondition or limitation: physical versions of "one bit buys one bit". Chandra's integrals read in discrete time as an entropy-rate difference h(y) \ensuremath{-} h(d), which ties this node to S1.13.

\textbf{Idea.} Two families of rate limits:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item The Bode integral read as entropy-rate conservation. For strictly proper L, the output entropy rate is H(y) = H(d) + \ensuremath{\Sigma} ln\textbar{}p\_unstable\textbar{} \ensuremath{\geq} H(d), Zhang 2003 Thm 1, and a biproper L can go below. With a channel, h\ensuremath{\infty}(e) \ensuremath{-} h\ensuremath{\infty}(d) \ensuremath{\geq} \ensuremath{\Sigma} log\textbar{}\ensuremath{\lambda}\_unstable\textbar{} \ensuremath{-} C, Martins--Dahleh. Zang 2003 and Iglesias 2001 give nonlinear and time-varying versions; Chen 2019 reviews them.
\item Minimal data rates for stabilisation or for reaching a cost: R \textgreater{} \ensuremath{\Sigma} log\textbar{}\ensuremath{\lambda}\_unstable\textbar{}, Nair--Evans 2004 and Tatikonda--Mitter 2004; rate--cost tradeoffs, Kostina--Hassibi; stabilisation over a Gaussian channel as a feedback code, Elia 2004; zero-delay quantiser coding with deterministic stationary optima, Ghomi 2020.
\item Estimation-side information flow. Mitter--Newton 2005 has no controller: observations lower H(X\textbar{}Y) by C(t) = I(X\_t; Y\_[0,t]), with the filter as a Maxwell demon. Feng 1997 gives zero mutual information between error and observations.
\item Capacity-capped regulation. Sims 2003 minimises E(Y \ensuremath{-} X)\textsuperscript{2} subject to I(X;Y) \textless{} C, an error-controlled min H(Z) with h(Z) = h(X) \ensuremath{-} C at the Gaussian optimum, deterministic when H(X) \ensuremath{\leq} C. Tanaka 2018 minimises directed information under an LQG cost and gets a randomised optimum, because that information cost is convex.
\item Absorbed S1.14-thermodynamic-and-biological-costs [L]: Information is the currency for reducing entropy:
\item feedback thermodynamics, \ensuremath{\langle}W\ensuremath{\rangle} \ensuremath{\geq} \ensuremath{\Delta}F \ensuremath{-} kT\ensuremath{\langle}I\ensuremath{\rangle}, Sagawa--Ueda, and S(\ensuremath{\rho}\_i) \ensuremath{-} S(\ensuremath{\rho}\_f) \ensuremath{\leq} I(\ensuremath{\rho}\_1 : X), Sagawa 2008;
\item continuous-state closed-loop entropy bounds, Delvenne 2013;
\item molecular noise suppression with its quartic-root law, Lestas; homeostatic variance bounded below by transfer entropy, Ito 2015;
\item biological robustness--fragility tradeoffs, Biswas--Iglesias, and glycolytic oscillations as Bode and Poisson log-sensitivity integrals, Chandra 2011, eqs.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Family S1.F5} Bode integral as entropy conservation, 20 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Iglesias-tradeoffs01}; \citealp{Zang-nonlinear03}; \citealp{Zhang-bode03}; \citealp{Elia-bode04}; \citealp{Martins-fundamental07}; \citealp{Martins-feedback08}; \citealp{Chen-fundamental19}; \citealp{Bode-relations40}; \citealp{Freudenberg-right85}; \citealp{Seron-fundamental97}; \citealp{Iglesias-analogue01}; \citealp{Zhang-hinfinity03}; \citealp{Zhang-information03}; \citealp{Mehta-markov08}; \citealp{Okano-characterization09}; \citealp{Yu-bode10}; \citealp{Ishii-achievable11}; \citealp{Li-bode13}; \citealp{Fang-design17}; \citealp{Fang-tradeoffs17}}}

\medskip\textsc{V. Controlling the outcome law}

\subsection{S1.16 minimum-entropy-control}\label{node:S1-S1-16-minimum-entropy-control}
\textit{Merged from} S1.15-minimum-variance (12); S1.19-fully-probabilistic-design (9). \textbf{Relation} $=$/$\subset$/$-$: Same objective in engineering form, but myopic. Yue 2006 is our concave-vertex argument, static, with one linear constraint; the controllers remain one-step Lyapunov designs. [S1.15-minimum-variance] Special case: the Gaussian surrogate. For linear-Gaussian loops minimum-entropy control equals minimum-variance control. [S1.19-fully-probabilistic-design] - FPD itself has the opposite sign: D(f\ensuremath{\|}\textsuperscript{I}f) = E\_f[\ensuremath{-}ln \textsuperscript{I}f] \ensuremath{-} H(f) rewards policy entropy, and the target is fixed. DR-FREE is a robust version of the same sign.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Lefebvre is a tool we use, MM, applied to a different objective.
\end{itemize}


\textbf{Idea.} The Wang school minimises J = H(y) + u\textsuperscript{T}Ru, or an error entropy, for non-Gaussian stochastic systems, one step at a time with B-spline PDF models.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Wang 2002, eq. 17: J(u\_k) = \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\int}\ensuremath{\gamma} ln \ensuremath{\gamma} dy + R u\_k\textsuperscript{2}. Eq. 27 shows the entropy term is concave in u, so R must be large: a one-step vertex effect suppressed by the control penalty.
\item Yue 2003 minimises the entropy of \ensuremath{\gamma}\_e conditioned on the information vector \ensuremath{\phi}\_k and u\_k, which is the one-step conditional H(e\_k \textbar{} \ensuremath{\phi}\_k, u\_k), our H(Z\textbar{}S,A) at each s, not the marginal. A mean term does the I(S;Z) work.
\item Yue 2006: the PDF is linear in the B-spline weights, so H is concave in them and minima lie on the boundary; under a mean constraint the minimum uses at most two basis functions.
\item Ren 2012 uses R\'enyi-2 entropy through the information potential; Zhou 2018 builds entropy benchmarks for performance assessment; Wang 2021 surveys the school.
\item Absorbed S1.15-minimum-variance [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Minimise output variance, the second-moment ancestor.
\item Absorbed S1.19-fully-probabilistic-design [\ensuremath{-}]: K\'arn\'y minimises D(f\ensuremath{\|}\textsuperscript{I}f), the KL divergence from the closed-loop law to an ideal law.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Wang-minimum02}: Our objective in engineering form, read from library copy: J=-int gamma ln gamma + R u\^{}2 one-step (eq 17); entropy term concave in u (eq 27), so a vertex effect is suppressed by a large control penalty
\item \citet{Yue-minimum06}: Promoted: only minimum-entropy-control paper with vertex theory; H concave in B-spline weights, boundary minima, at most two basis functions under a mean constraint, Thms 8-9; static only
\item \citet{Karny-fully06}: Opposite-sign neighbour: KL to an ideal closed-loop law rewards policy entropy, optimum is a randomised Gibbs law; also in B8
\item \citet{Lefebvre-probabilistic24}: Closest algorithmic precedent: MM fixed-point iterations for, risk-sensitive, optimal control converging to deterministic policies
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S1.17 prescribed-target-law}\label{node:S1-S1-17-prescribed-target-law}
\textbf{Relation} $\approx$: Same mechanism, different objective. The target is fixed, so the problem is convex; ours chooses the law and is concave. Lozano-Dur\'an is the one member whose target moves: a refit-then-steer loop with a KL objective, structurally close to our MM refit step. The "cooling" steady-state problem is the closest continuous analogue, and Sinkhorn is a tool for occupancy-marginal constraints.

\textbf{Idea.} Make the state or output law match a \emph{given} target:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item B-spline and Fokker--Planck PDF shaping, Wang; Annunziato;
\item stationary PDF assignment, Forbes;
\item Schr\"odinger-bridge and optimal-transport density steering, Chen--Georgiou--Pavon and Beghi;
\item covariance control, Hotz--Skelton; Okamoto;
\item Markov-chain synthesis for swarm densities, A\c{c}\i{}kme\c{s}e, Bandyopadhyay;
\item Lozano-Dur\'an 2021: min KL(J \ensuremath{\|} \^J) against a moment-rescaled target that is refit each round, eqs. 99--103, plus max I(J;S) and I(J;A). The Wang 2021 survey covers both branches of the school: target tracking belongs here, and its target-free entropy minimisation, Sec. 4 eq. 13, belongs to S1.16.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Family S1.F7} covariance steering and Schroedinger bridges, 18 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Hotz-covariance87}; \citealp{Beghi-relative96}; \citealp{Chen-fast15}; \citealp{Chen-optimal16a}; \citealp{Okamoto-optimal18}; \citealp{Chen-optimal21}; \citealp{Chen-stochastic21}; \citealp{Dai-stochastic91}; \citealp{Pavon-free91}; \citealp{Grigoriadis-minimum97}; \citealp{Chen-steering15}; \citealp{Chen-optimal16b}; \citealp{Chen-relation15}; \citealp{Chen-optimal17}; \citealp{Goldshtein-finite17}; \citealp{Bakolas-finite18}; \citealp{Chen-optimal18}; \citealp{Liu-optimal25}}}

\subsection{S1.18 risk-sensitive}\label{node:S1-S1-18-risk-sensitive}
\textbf{Relation} $\approx$: Same mechanism, different objective. It regulates the spread of \emph{cost}, not the entropy of an outcome. "Minimum entropy H\ensuremath{\infty}" is a name clash, not our objective.

\textbf{Idea.} Exponential-of-cost criteria penalise the cumulants of cost:
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item LEQG, Jacobson and Whittle: \ensuremath{\gamma}(\ensuremath{\theta}) = \ensuremath{-}(2/\ensuremath{\theta}) log E exp(\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\theta}G) \ensuremath{\approx} EG \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{\theta} Var G/2, with a deterministic linear optimum that is noise-dependent;
\item equivalence to minimum-entropy H\ensuremath{\infty} control, Glover--Doyle and Mustafa;
\item the variational form log E\_\ensuremath{\mu} e\^{}\ensuremath{\psi} = sup\_\ensuremath{\nu}[\ensuremath{\int}\ensuremath{\psi} d\ensuremath{\nu} \ensuremath{-} h(\ensuremath{\nu}\textbar{}\ensuremath{\mu})], a game against KL-penalised nature, Dai Pra 1996 and Petersen 2000;
\item sensorimotor evidence, Nagengast 2010.
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{VI. Occupancy-level optimality}

\subsection{S1.20 deterministic-optimality-over-occupancies}\label{node:S1-S1-20-deterministic-optimality-over-occupancies}
\textbf{Relation} $\supset$: Derman and Piunovskiy are a \textbf{generalisation} of our vertex step: ours is Derman's corollary with f(d) = H\_\ensuremath{\mu}[dK], concave because d \ensuremath{\mapsto} dK is linear. The variance papers are \textbf{same mechanism, different objective}, and the Gaussian case links them to ours. Mannor, Haskell and the constraint theorems are \textbf{precondition or limitation}.

\textbf{Idea.} A concave functional of the occupancy, minimised over MDP policies, has a deterministic vertex optimum.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item General theorems. Derman 1970, the Application after Thm 2: any continuous concave f of the long-run expected state--action frequencies is minimised by a deterministic stationary policy; Thm 3 gives stationary randomised policies under constraints. Piunovskiy 1998, after Feinberg 1982: any criterion with R(barycentre) \ensuremath{\geq} mean of R, that is concave under mixing, has a selector no worse than any policy; extreme strategic measures are selectors, Thm 3.
\item Variance, a concave function of the occupancy. Kawai 1987: the stationary one-transition variance \ensuremath{\Sigma} R x \ensuremath{-} (\ensuremath{\Sigma} r x)\textsuperscript{2} is concave in x. Filar 1989: a deterministic optimum for average and discount-normalised mean--variance, Cor. 2.1 and 4.1; only the stagewise case loses stationarity, not determinism. Sobel 1994, Prop. 2: the optimum is an extreme point, and Comment 6 extends this to any quasiconcave variability measure. Xia 2016, filed in S4, to Xia 2026: Var = min\_y E(r \ensuremath{-} y)\textsuperscript{2} gives a refit/solve loop whose fixed points are local optima, and a global algorithm.
\item Huang 1994: a parametric LP for objectives with one nonlinear direction, which solves min H[d\^{}\ensuremath{\pi} K] exactly when \textbar{}Z\textbar{} = 2.
\item Collins 1997: a strictly convex function of the final-state law maximised at a Markov deterministic vertex, a particular case.
\item Limits. With N constraints the optimum mixes N+1 selectors, Piunovskiy Thms 9--10; two pure policies under a mean constraint, Kawai Thm 1 and Sobel Prop. 2; Mannor 2013 for finite-horizon variance; Haskell 2013, whose stochastic-dominance constraint is linear in the occupancy, so the LP optimum is stationary randomised.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Sobel-meanvariance94}: Promoted: stationary variance vx-(rx)\^{}2 concave in Derman occupancy x; Prop 2 extreme-point optimum, edge under a mean constraint; Comment 6 extends to any quasiconcave variability measure
\item \citet{Piunovskiy-controlled98}: Promoted: Lemma 3 + Thm 4, any criterion concave under mixing has a selector no worse than any policy; Thms 9-10, N constraints need N+1 selectors
\item \citet{Mannor-algorithmic13}: Limitation: a mean constraint makes randomised policies strictly better; our determinism claim needs the unconstrained or tilted form
\end{itemize}

\section{S2 Homeostasis, surprise minimisation and viability}
308 papers in 10 nodes: 18 core, 146 support, 144 background; 11 families hold 142 papers.

\medskip\textsc{I. Set-point costs on an internal level, aimed, no entropy term,}

\subsection{S2.02 drive-reduction-reward}\label{node:S2-S2-02-drive-reduction-reward}
\textit{Merged from} S2.01-classical-homeostasis (6); S2.03-survival-probability-reward (4). \textbf{Relation} $\subset$: Special case: the zero-entropy-weight case of our tilted objective, which is our MM inner step with q frozen at e\^{}\{\ensuremath{-}c\} and never refit. Two derived consequences: under the average-reward criterion the drive difference has gain zero, so only the level cost matters; with termination at a viability edge the difference reward charges the dead state its terminal drive, while a plain level reward with zero after death pays the agent to die. - **What \citet{Yoshida-emergence24} adds.\emph{* It compares \ensuremath{\beta}[D\_t \ensuremath{-} D\_\{t+1\}], \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\beta}D\_t, \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\beta}D\_t + b, a terminal-only \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\beta}, and +1 per food, with D = \ensuremath{\|}x \ensuremath{-} x}\ensuremath{\|}\textsuperscript{2}. The three dense forms share one optimum by scale, shift and shaping with \ensuremath{\Phi} = \ensuremath{-}D/(1\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\gamma}). That holds only on a continuing chain: the authors withhold done signals for the dense forms. Shaped reward wins on survival time; the sparse and food rewards fail. Its Appendix D writes KL(P\_\ensuremath{\pi}\ensuremath{\|}P*)/T = \ensuremath{-}J\_h \ensuremath{-} S(\ensuremath{\pi})/T \ensuremath{\leq} \ensuremath{-}J\_h, so trajectory entropy S(\ensuremath{\pi}) enters with the sign opposite to ours. [S2.01-classical-homeostasis] Special case for Hull and Sibly: a cost on a fixed outcome around a fixed set point. Cannon is the nearest classic to a distributional criterion. He asks for a small spread and names no target. That is the setting that motivated Conant and Ashby's H(Z), but none of these works writes a law-level objective. [S2.03-survival-probability-reward] Special case, and the same vertex mechanism. Log survival probability is our Gibbs reward with q fixed to the survival curve. Moreno-Bote is the closest published statement that the good homeostat is deterministic, but his range version becomes maximum occupancy, with the opposite sign.

\textbf{Idea.} Reward r\_t = D(h\_t) \ensuremath{-} D(h\_\{t+1\}), with D a convex distance to a set point h*. With \ensuremath{\Phi} = \ensuremath{-}D this is r\_t = \ensuremath{-}(1\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\gamma})D(h\_\{t+1\}) + [\ensuremath{\gamma}\ensuremath{\Phi}(h\_\{t+1\}) \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{\Phi}(h\_t)], a level cost plus potential-based shaping. Summed, \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_t \ensuremath{\gamma}\^{}t(D\_t \ensuremath{-} D\_\{t+1\}) = D\_0 \ensuremath{-} ((1\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\gamma})/\ensuremath{\gamma}) \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_\{t\ensuremath{\geq}1\} \ensuremath{\gamma}\^{}t D\_t, Keramati Eq S5; \citet{Laurencon-continuous24} Eq 8; Hulme Eq 8. Keramati and Hulme also read D as \ensuremath{-}ln p(h) with p \ensuremath{\propto} e\^{}\{\ensuremath{-}D\}, so the expected drive is a cross-entropy against a fixed density. Deep variants run PPO or DQN on MuJoCo bodies, vision, a quadruped and nutrition geometry.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.01-classical-homeostasis [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Cannon keeps disturbances "within narrow limits" and prevents wide oscillations.
\item Absorbed S2.03-survival-probability-reward [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Maximise P(alive at all t).
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Keramati-homeostatic14}: Drive-reduction reward; discounted drive difference = D\_0 - ((1-gamma)/gamma) sum gamma\^{}t D\_t, i.e. potential shaping of a set-point cost; D read as -ln p with p fixed = our frozen-q step
\item \citet{Yoshida-emergence24}: promoted: the one comparison of homeostatic reward forms, now read; dense forms share an optimum only on a continuing chain; App D puts trajectory entropy with the opposite sign
\item \citet{MorenoBote-homeostasis26}: Closest paper: homeostasis as max p(y=y\_d) is linear in each pi\_t so a deterministic optimum exists and the free-energy surrogate is strictly suboptimal; range version becomes MOP, opposite sign,
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S2.04 allostasis}\label{node:S2-S2-04-allostasis}
\textit{Merged from} S2.06-interoception-and-affect (13). \textbf{Relation} $\approx$/\textsf{T}: Same mechanism, different objective. A moving reference is what our MM loop does when it refits q to the policy's own outcome law. Most members move a point target. Sterling's Fig. 4 adapts a response to a law of loads, a density-level adjustment, but no paper charges a spread. Sterling also constrains the choice of Z: low entropy of the raw milieu is pathology, so Z must be the survival-relevant error, not the physiological parameter. [S2.06-interoception-and-affect] Tool we use: this is our read-out channel K made internal; the HRRL orosensory estimate is a learned K.

\textbf{Idea.} Regulation by prediction. Sterling states the goal "is not to preserve constancy". Blood-pressure fluctuation arises "from precise control", and response curves adapt so that their steep part sits on the distribution of probable loads (Fig. 4). Schulkin and Sterling make every apparent set point a moving mean of predicted need. Ramsay and Woods split the vocabulary: an adjustable set point is rheostasis, anticipation is homeostatic, and allostasis is reserved for dysregulation by overcorrection, persistent effectors or concurrent opposing effectors. HRRL argues discounting already gives allostatic behaviour. Nguy poses fast regulation to a reference plus slow revision of the reference. \citet{Keramati-cocaine17} keeps the Keramati14 reward and lets the set point drift up with each dose.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.06-interoception-and-affect [T]: The internal variable is read through an interoceptive channel, and affect is the evaluation of that reading, Craig, Seth, Damasio, Ca\~namero, interoceptive AI.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S2.05 multiple-needs-and-coupled-drives}\label{node:S2-S2-05-multiple-needs-and-coupled-drives}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation. An additive cost \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_i c\_i decouples needs; a joint entropy H(Z\_1,\dots{},Z\_k) = \ensuremath{\Sigma} H(Z\_i) \ensuremath{-} TC couples them through total correlation. No paper has such a coupling term. Rolf is the opposite curvature: it maximises a concave function of returns, which rewards balance and a stochastic mixture, where we minimise a concave function of the occupancy, which rewards a vertex. Rolf also shows that a nonlinear objective breaks Markov sufficiency of s. Our pooled entropy raises the same question.

\textbf{Idea.} Several internal variables, one body. Urgency-weighted per-drive rewards, Konidaris and Barto, summed modular critics, Dulberg, reward bases reweighted by need, Millidge, drive competition and hormone-like modulation. Rolf's MORE maximises V = \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\Sigma}\_k exp(\ensuremath{-}V\_k), a concave soft-min over per-need returns, and its optimal policy is Markov only in (s, w) with deficit weights w\_k(t+1) = w\_k(t)\^{}\ensuremath{\gamma} exp(\ensuremath{-}r\_k(t)). Across bodies, a partner's drive is added to one's own, D = D\_self + w D\_partner (Yoshida and Man, w = 0.5).

\medskip\textsc{II. Regulation of the learner itself}

\subsection{S2.07 learner-homeostasis-ultrastability}\label{node:S2-S2-07-learner-homeostasis-ultrastability}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation, and a contrast. The regulated quantity is a property of the learner, not the outcome. Ashby 1952 supplies three things we use: the criterion is a viability box, R has no edge to the essential variables except through the environment, our fixed K, the no-wireheading condition, and learning pays only when disturbances are constrained and recurrent, our persistent-disturbance requirement. Exploration triggered by loss of homeostasis is the dual of our belief-state exploration.

\textbf{Idea.} Homeostasis of the controller. Ashby's ultrastability resets parameters at random when an essential variable leaves its limits. Di Paolo's plasticity is triggered when neurons leave a firing band. Lones makes receptor gains a positive-feedback loop driven by essential-variable error, a continuous ultrastability that does not cite Ashby. Ikegami replaces the random search by membrane shape and by chaotic Daisyworld dynamics, and exploration starts when the homeostatic attractor breaks. Later work adapts PPO's learning rate and entropy coefficient to affect-like set points, or treats boredom as homeostasis of cognitive resources.

\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Ashby-design52}: Founding (1952): survival = essential variables kept inside limits, a band not an entropy; ultrastability; S. 10/2-10/4 is our persistent-disturbance requirement; owner B1
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{III. Bands, survival and death}

\subsection{S2.09 death-as-absorbing-outcome}\label{node:S2-S2-09-death-as-absorbing-outcome}
\textit{Merged from} S2.08-viability-constraints (23). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}/$\subset$: Precondition or limitation. Our Gibbs reward ln q \ensuremath{-} c \ensuremath{\leq} 0 is a cost \ensuremath{-}E\_K ln \ensuremath{\hat{q}} + c \ensuremath{\geq} 0. With a cost-free absorbing dead state, Bertsekas's properness then means the optimum dies with probability 1, so it is death-seeking in standard episodic code. With a dead symbol in Z and w = P(alive), H\_\ensuremath{\mu} = h\_b(w) + w H\_\ensuremath{\mu}(living) + (1 \ensuremath{-} w) c(dead). Under the average-reward criterion with absorbing death, H\_\ensuremath{\mu} \ensuremath{\to} c(dead), so survival wins only if c(dead) exceeds the best living H\_\ensuremath{\mu}, a threshold like Massiani's p*. This decomposition is a B16 derivation; no paper states it. [S2.08-viability-constraints] Special case. Our viability box is a reference measure infinite outside K, and Fisac's hard box is its limit. Survival-probability objectives are the w-only part of our death decomposition. Aubin Thm 4.10.4 writes an occupational cost as the infimum of a linear functional over a closed convex set of generalised occupational measures, the set-valued twin of our occupancy polytope. \citet{Sun-safe21} Prop 1 is a reward-form threshold like p*: absorbing death with a small enough reward makes the ET-MDP optimum equal the CMDP optimum, but exactly only for a binary absorbing cost set.

\textbf{Idea.} Termination is an absorbing state of fixed value, usually 0. That silently sets a reference point: positive rewards become a survival bonus and negative rewards a suicide incentive, Martin, Everitt and Hutter, Thm 8; Kostrikov, the log D case. Bertsekas and Tsitsiklis give the formal frame: with a cost-free absorbing terminal and improper policies costing +\ensuremath{\infty}, a stationary proper optimum exists, Prop 2. Fixes: timeouts are not deaths, Pardo; resets make an ergodic chain with J\_epi = J\_avg E[T] (Bojun, White's transition-based \ensuremath{\gamma} = 0, Zhou's stochastic resetting); reward centring, which Naik Sec. 6 says removes the r(\ensuremath{\pi})/(1\ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\gamma}) offset only in continuing chains, so it fixes death bias only after death is made a \ensuremath{\gamma} = 0 transition. Under admissible pessimism in offline RL, truncation is valued at the floor, so zero, random or negated rewards still yield survival, Li Prop 1, Thm 1, and length bias is the data trace of absorbing death.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.08-viability-constraints [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Keep the state in a set forever instead of at a point: Aubin's viability kernel, HJ reachability, discounted safety Bellman equations, a measure of viable actions, and failure penalties that are exact above a finite threshold p*, Massiani 2023, Thm 2.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Martin-death16}: Thm 8: non-positive rewards with a zero-reward sink make agents seek death; our Gibbs reward ln q - c \textless{}= 0 is exactly this case
\item \citet{Massiani-safe23}: Finite failure-penalty threshold, Thm 2: the theory for making death an explicit, costly outcome
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{IV. The opposite sign inside the band}

\subsection{S2.10 path-entropy-mop}\label{node:S2-S2-10-path-entropy-mop}
\textit{Merged from} S2.11-causal-entropic-and-future-options (19). \textbf{Relation} $-$: Opposite sign. Most members maximise conditional or path entropies inside the viable set. Grytskyy is the exact mirror of our root: its \ensuremath{\beta} H(S) is H(d\^{}\ensuremath{\pi} K) with K the identity, on the same polytope. Thm 2.1: R\_p is concave iff \ensuremath{\alpha}, \ensuremath{\beta} \ensuremath{\geq} 0. Thm 2.2: the optimum is unique, interior and stochastic, \ensuremath{\pi}* \ensuremath{\propto} e\^{}\{A/\ensuremath{\alpha}\}, found through the convex dual log Z\_V. Maximising the concave functional gives an interior point; minimising it gives a vertex. A KL toward p\_0 enters as \ensuremath{\beta} log p\_0, which is our MM reward ln \ensuremath{\hat{q}} with \ensuremath{\hat{q}} frozen. Both signs keep the agent alive and away from absorbing edges; MOP's agent keeps "dancing" while ours collapses to a deterministic policy. The hazard flips: MOP needs a low-entropy death, and Kiefer notes that a physically high-entropy death should attract MOP agents. For \ensuremath{\beta} \textgreater{} 0 MOP agents are drawn to a noisy room, Ramirez-Ruiz Fig. 2e; noise that reaches Z repels ours. [S2.11-causal-entropic-and-future-options] Opposite sign. Kappen 2013 shows path entropy is a sum of conditional entropies, so with state-independent noise it produces behaviour only through absorbing walls. Mann's Galton-Watson futures tree gets cohesion only from extinct subtrees, which is Kappen's point. Our marginal needs no wall. Empowerment is +I(A;Z\textbar{}S), the middle term of our budget with the other sign.

\textbf{Idea.} Maximum occupancy: maximise \ensuremath{\alpha} H(A\textbar{}s) + \ensuremath{\beta} H(S\ensuremath{'}\textbar{}s,a) summed along the path, with absorbing death. Range homeostasis is recast as MOP, Moreno-Bote 2026, and entropy-regularised RL with failure penalties prefers states with many viable future actions, Massiani 2025. Grytskyy generalises MOP to the stationary occupancy: R\_p = E\_p r + \ensuremath{\alpha} H(A\textbar{}S) + \ensuremath{\beta} H(S) over the linear flow polytope. Here the \ensuremath{\beta} term is the marginal entropy of the long-run state law, not a path entropy. Savas maximises MDP path entropy under a temporal-logic constraint.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.11-causal-entropic-and-future-options [\ensuremath{-}]: Maximise the entropy of reachable futures, causal entropic forces, the count of reachable states, future state maximisation, or empowerment, and read survival and centring as a by-product.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Ramirez-Ruiz-complex22}: MOP: maximise action and transition entropy inside a viability set with absorbing death; the opposite-sign twin of our objective; noisy-room trap for beta \textgreater{} 0; owner B6
\item \citet{Grytskyy-general23}: promoted: exact mirror of the root, max E r + a H(A\textbar{}S) + b H(S) over the same flow polytope; Thm 2.1 concave, Thm 2.2 unique interior optimum
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{V. Surprise and predictability of the agent's own stream}

\subsection{S2.12 within-episode-surprise-minimisation}\label{node:S2-S2-12-within-episode-surprise-minimisation}
\textit{Merged from} S2.13-min-max-and-homeo-heterostatic (7); S2.14-entropy-rate-predictability (9); S2.14a-trajectory-compression (8); S2.16-entropy-of-another-variable (6). \textbf{Relation} $=$/$\approx$/$\sim$/\textsf{L}: Same objective in its reward, different time structure. SMiRL's density is refit within each episode, so it bounds trajectory entropy, not occupancy entropy. It drops I(E;S) from H(d) = E\_E H(d\_E) + I(E;S). IC2's headline reward \ensuremath{-}KL(q\_t\ensuremath{\|}\ensuremath{\bar{q}}) penalises only the across-time part and pays a bonus for belief entropy. The persistent disturbance we require is SMiRL's unstable environment. Ovalle is the closest member to a read-out channel K. Suri's EMIX "surprise" is a learned log-sum-exp score of state spread, not a log density, so it is a nominal member only. [S2.13-min-max-and-homeo-heterostatic] Same mechanism, different objective. AS Lemma 2, H(d(o)) = E H(O\textbar{}s) + H(d(s)), is our budget on block MDPs. Yu fits a marginal Q(s\ensuremath{'}\textbar{}s) and then updates the policy against it, whose expectation is I(S\ensuremath{'};A\textbar{}S); alternating the two collapses the policy to a point mass. That is our MM structure and our deterministic end point, read as a pathology. MOSS is the only minimiser that pools over the replay buffer, and only as a skill. PRIME, once novelty is resolved, minimises H(Z\textbar{}S,A) and stays blind to I(S;Z). [S2.14-entropy-rate-predictability] Same sign, different variable. The rate is I(A;Z\textbar{}S) + H(Z\textbar{}S,A) and PARL's surrogate is H(Z\textbar{}S,A); both are linear in the occupancy and blind to I(S;Z), so a predictable 2-cycle costs 0 for PARL and ln 2 for us. PMA minimises H(Z\textbar{}S,A) but maximises I(A;Z\textbar{}S). van Zutphen is a special case of our vertex argument. The path entropy splits as H(X\_0) + \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_k E H(X\_\{k+1\}\textbar{}X\_k). The backup \ensuremath{\Phi}(p,V) = \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\Sigma} p log p + \ensuremath{\Sigma} p V is concave in p, Lemma 3, so \ensuremath{\Phi}(\ensuremath{\Sigma}\_a \ensuremath{\pi}\_a p\^{}a, V) \ensuremath{\geq} \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_a \ensuremath{\pi}\_a \ensuremath{\Phi}(p\^{}a, V) and a deterministic policy is robustly optimal, Thm 2. It is our concavity-to-determinism step made per state, and it never reaches the pooled occupancy. [S2.14a-trajectory-compression] Same mechanism, different objective: MM with a refit prior, but on a conditional entropy of a learned latent or of actions. Precedent for learned outcomes, and TERL is a soft H(A\textbar{}S) = 0. [S2.16-entropy-of-another-variable] Precondition or limitation, as a choice of channel. LLM entropy minimisation minimises per-context H(A\textbar{}S), the term the good-regulator theorem drives to zero, used as the whole objective. Single-scene entropy is a plain reward, linear in the occupancy.

\textbf{Idea.} Reward log p\_\{\ensuremath{\theta}\_\{t\ensuremath{-}1\}\}(s\_t) under a density refit to the current episode, with the state augmented by (\ensuremath{\theta}\_t, t), SMiRL. SMiRL bounds \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_t H(s\_t) by \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\Sigma}\_t E log p\_\{\ensuremath{\theta}\_\{t\ensuremath{-}1\}\}(s\_t) and needs an "unstable" environment or it finds a trivial low-entropy policy. IC2 does the same on a learned belief. SOFE generalises the state augmentation that makes such bonuses stationary. Ovalle puts the surprisal on a designated outcome bit v, here in-task viability, rather than on the state. Kaiser's evolved swarms score the mean next-sensor prediction accuracy over one episode; the variant with all predictions fixed is a frozen reference.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.13-min-max-and-homeo-heterostatic [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Pair a surprise-minimising part with a surprise-maximising part to escape both the dark room and the noisy TV: an adversarial game on one body, Adversarial Surprise, a fixed half-episode skill mixture, MOSS, a bandit that picks the sign per episode, S-Adapt, or a homeostatic familiarity term plus a heterostatic curiosity term, Yu, Chang and Kanai; Magrans de Abril and Kanai.
\item Absorbed S2.14-entropy-rate-predictability [\ensuremath{\sim}]: Minimise the entropy rate lim (1/T) H(X\_\{0:T\}) traded against reward, PARL, a finite path entropy, interval MDPs, or model error, PMA.
\item Absorbed S2.14a-trajectory-compression [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Pay for the bits of the agent's own trajectory, refunded by a learned predictive prior: RPC's reward log m(z\ensuremath{'}\textbar{}z,a) \ensuremath{-} log \ensuremath{\phi}(z\ensuremath{'}\textbar{}s\ensuremath{'}); TERL minimises H(a\_\{1:T\}\textbar{}z\_\{1:T\}) through an inverse-dynamics predictor; SPAC's cost log \ensuremath{\pi}(a\textbar{}s) \ensuremath{-} log \ensuremath{\phi}(a\textbar{}a\_past) with a jointly refit transformer prior, and LZ-SAC as its fixed-reference variant; total correlation of latent-action trajectories, of which RPC is a special case (\citet{You-maximum25}, App.
\item Absorbed S2.16-entropy-of-another-variable [L]: Low entropy of a different variable: the model's own outputs per input (Tent, LLM entropy minimisation with reward log \ensuremath{\pi}(y)), the relations inside one scene, RaIR, or low state entropy measured on skilled balance.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Berseth-smirl21}: Only published reward that is literally our Gibbs reward ln q with q refit; but refit per episode, so it bounds trajectory entropy and drops I(E;S)
\item \citet{Rhinehart-information21}: IC2: minimise visitation entropy of a learned belief, within episode; within-episode analogue of our belief-MDP argument; headline reward flips the noise term to a bonus
\item \citet{Yu-boredom18}: Fit marginal Q(s'\textbar{}s), then act: expectation is I(S';A\textbar{}S), alternating updates collapse the policy to a point mass; same MM structure and end point, read as pathology
\item \citet{Fickinger-explore21}: Adversarial surprise: Lemma 2 H(d(o)) = E H(O\textbar{}s) + H(d(s)) is our budget on block MDPs; min-max sign switching as an empirical dark-room fix
\item \citet{vanZutphen-predictable24}: promoted: Lemma 3 plus Thm 2 is our concavity-to-deterministic-vertex step, per state on path entropy, robust over interval transitions
\item \citet{JarneOrnia-predictable25}: PARL: entropy-rate minimisation, linear in occupancy; blind to I(S;Z) so a predictable 2-cycle costs 0 for them and ln 2 for us
\end{itemize}

\subsection{S2.15 predictive-information}\label{node:S2-S2-15-predictive-information}
\textit{Merged from} S2.15a-homeokinesis (7). \textbf{Relation} $-$/$=$: Opposite sign on the marginal, same sign on the rate. Ay 2008 Eq 22 and Ay 2012 Eq 18 write predictive information as the marginal entropy minus a noise term that does not depend on the policy, so the policy only raises what we lower. Zahedi splits one-step PI into diversity H(S\ensuremath{'}) minus compliance H(S\ensuremath{'}\textbar{}S) and states that cart-pole balancing minimises sensor entropy, so it contradicts PI. Little and Sommer argue in exactly these terms that maximal mutual information, not minimal entropy, escapes the dark room. [S2.15a-homeokinesis] Same objective in its founding form, then opposite sign. Homeokinesis met the dark room two decades before the RL surprise line, 1999 and 2002 against SMiRL 2019 to 2021. None of the full texts of SMiRL, AS, IC2, MOSS, S-Adapt, PARL, SOFE or Yu cites Der or Martius. Our route keeps the minimising sign and adds a disturbance and a reference. Polyhomeostasis is a fixed-reference relative whose KL(p\ensuremath{\|}q*) = \ensuremath{-}H(p) \ensuremath{-} E\_p ln q* rewards entropy.

\textbf{Idea.} Maximise I(past; future) of the sensor stream, which equals H(S) \ensuremath{-} H(S\textbar{}S\_past): variety that stays predictable, as a drive for robot self-organisation. Still 2009 maximises I(\{s,a\}; z) minus complexity costs. Ay 2012 extends Ay 2008 to n dimensions: I(S\_\{t+1\};S\_t) = \ensuremath{\tfrac12} ln\textbar{}\ensuremath{\Sigma}\textbar{} \ensuremath{-} \ensuremath{\tfrac12} ln\textbar{}D\textbar{}, with the noise covariance D independent of the controller C.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S2.15a-homeokinesis [=]: Adapt a self-model and the controller together to minimise the model's prediction error.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Ay-predictive08}: Predictive information = marginal entropy minus policy-independent noise term (Eq 22): maximises what we minimise; the principled opposite-sign branch of homeokinesis
\item \citet{Der-homeokinesis99}: Founding homeokinesis: minimising self-model prediction error stabilises the state at zero, the dark room two decades before SMiRL
\item \citet{Der-true02}: States that every predictability objective has the trivial do-nothing solution and fixes it by flipping sign, time-loop error; the RL surprise line does not cite it
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{VI. Regulation that emerges}

\subsection{S2.17 daisyworld-gaia}\label{node:S2-S2-17-daisyworld-gaia}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation, as the emergent pole. A level is regulated, the set point is implied by the growth peak, and no agent optimises anything. Where an optimisation appears it is a closure or a description, and it maximises: Toniazzo closes the heat fluxes by maximum entropy production, which keeps only the two maximum-contrast species; Ackland finds the time-averaged 2-D state maximises the amount of life. Neither charges the law of the regulated temperature.

\textbf{Idea.} Agents with peaked growth curves in a variable they also perturb hold it near constant under rising forcing. In Watson and Lovelock the coexistence steady state forces equal growth rates and pins each type's local temperature, "despite \dots{} no physically real reference temperature". Weber derives T\_eq = T\_opt \ensuremath{\pm} q(A\_w \ensuremath{-} A\_b)/2, independent of solar forcing, so T\_opt enters the heat budget as a fixed set point. That set point must be anchored. If it relaxes freely toward the local temperature, regulation is lost, Lenton and Lovelock 2000; a parabolic cost on drift restores it; and Williams and Noble need the growth optimum to adapt about 100 times slower than albedo. Weaver and Dyke add timescale conditions: regulation needs \ensuremath{\tau}\_\ensuremath{\gamma}/\ensuremath{\tau}\_L below a critical ratio. The selection debate asks how regulation survives cheaters: by-product regulation, selection by persistence, sequential selection, which Lenton and colleagues call satisficing, not optimising. Kirchner reads the data against Gaian regulation: the biotic climate feedback gain is positive. "Entropy" here means entropy production, configurational entropy, species-abundance entropy, or descriptive entropy of planetary time series.

\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Watson-biological83}: Founding Daisyworld: a level held near a set point that no agent aims at and nothing optimises; the emergent pole of the aimed-vs-emergent axis
\end{itemize}
\memb{Family S2.F7} Daisyworld models, 18 papers, 14 here: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Watson-biological83}; \citealp{LentonLovelock-daisyworld00}; \citealp{Weber-homeostasis01}; \citealp{Wood-daisyworld08}; \citealp{Maull-synthetic24}\dag; \citealp{Sowinski-exodaisy24}; \citealp{LovelockKump-failure94}\dag; \citealp{RobertsonRobinson-darwinian98}; \citealp{LentonLovelock-daisyworld01}\dag; \citealp{Ackland-catastrophic03}; \citealp{Wood-mutation06}; \citealp{NortoftNielsen-homeostasis09}\dag; \citealp{Kageyama-mechanisms20}; \citealp{Arnscheidt-rateinduced24}}}

\subsection{S2.18 rein-control-and-byproduct-regulation}\label{node:S2-S2-18-rein-control-and-byproduct-regulation}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}: Precondition or limitation. This node answers set point versus band. A band arises only when two opposing controllers' set points are ordered so they do not fight; with the reverse order, or with two uncoupled integral reins at different set points, the system winds up and never settles, and Saunders's unique point needs the mutual-inhibition coupling. Across all members the regulated quantity is a deterministic level, or at most its mean, and neither variance nor entropy is an objective. The many contingent attractors resemble our many vertex-local optima, but nothing is optimised.

\textbf{Idea.} A rein pulls but cannot push. Clynes 1969 pairs two unidirectionally rate-sensitive channels, a derivative plus a rectification, mostly sensory, with no set point, band or optimisation. The level-regulation reading is Saunders's. In Saunders 1998 two integral reins coupled by mutual inhibition give f(G*) = (D\_A/D\_B) c(G*): a set point fixed by the ratio of removal rates, independent of input and demand. Saunders 2000 generalises this: k steady-state equations in k functions fix the regulated state, and a set point sits where two growth curves cross, as in Koeslag's islet flip-flop. The antithetic controller pins the mean E[X] = \ensuremath{\mu}/\ensuremath{\theta} and leaves the variance unregulated, Briat; Gupta and Khammash. In Drengstig's antagonistic inflow and outflow motifs, a band arises when the inflow set point is below the outflow set point, and windup when it is above. Harvey 2004 and McDonald-Gibson get two-sided regulation from traits selection does not act on. Williams and Lenton's flask networks regulate a band [E\ensuremath{-}, E+], because selection acts only outside it. Dyke and Weaver find an expected (R/(2\ensuremath{\surd}(2\ensuremath{\pi})\ensuremath{\sigma}))\^{}N stable regulated states.

\section{S3 Free energy, active inference and information-theoretic accounts of action}
256 papers in 9 nodes: 20 core, 153 support, 83 background; 8 families hold 127 papers.

\medskip\textsc{Group A. Same sign: minimise the outcome marginal H(Z) or a surrogate of it}

\subsection{N1 fep-self-entropy}\label{node:S3-N1-fep-self-entropy}
\textit{Merged from} N1a fep-self-entropy/particular-physics (9); N1b fep-self-entropy/critiques (14); N2 self-refit-preferences (5); N3 dark-room (8). \textbf{Relation} $=$/\textsf{L}/$\approx$: Same objective, different optimiser. Friston--Ao 2012 \citep{Friston-free12b} Eq. 7 is a* = argmin H(X\textbar{}m); Eq. 9 drops the action-dependence of the density, so each step is our MM inner step with q frozen at the model and the refit missing. Friston 2012 in Entropy \citep{Friston-free12c} Def. 1, Eq. 7 states \ensuremath{\phi}\_R* = argmin H(S\textbar{}m) over a deterministic internal map, our objective with Z = S; determinism is assumed, "internal states are not subject to fluctuations", not derived. Its information-bottleneck form keeps only perception and drops H(S\textbar{}m), but S\_B* = H(R,S) = H(S) + H(R\textbar{}S) upper-bounds our objective with gap zero exactly when H(R\textbar{}S) = 0, the model condition. The 2006 paper has no entropy step: action minimises expected sensory surprisal \ensuremath{-}\ensuremath{\langle}ln p(y(a)\textbar{}\ensuremath{\theta})\ensuremath{\rangle}\_q (Eq. 3); the self-entropy reading enters in 2009 and 2012. Friston 2010, Box 1, is a = argmin F with the long-run-entropy gloss in prose. [N1a fep-self-entropy/particular-physics] Same objective and same sign: Friston 2019 states that minimising self-entropy minimises I(E;P); \citet{Friston-path23} Eq. 26 shows expected EFE equals I(sensory; autonomous paths), also minimised. \citet{Friston-free23} Eq. 39 gives E[G] = H[p(\ensuremath{\alpha} paths)], valid only for precise particles with H(\ensuremath{\alpha}\textbar{}\ensuremath{\eta},s) = 0 (Eq. 37), so the deterministic regulator is assumed; its Sec. 7 concedes nothing implies low self-entropy. The information-gain bonus appears only pointwise. [N1b fep-self-entropy/critiques] Precondition or limitation. They attack the derivation, never the entropy objective, which justifies stating ours normatively on the occupancy. Biehl 2021 gives counterexamples to the free-energy lemma (Eqs. 27 to 34) and shows the two blanket conditions are independent; it attacks the inference reading only. Kirchhoff 2021 is a defence of the FEP against the Sterelny and Clark challenge, filed here as the reply; its good-regulator gloss is loose, since H(A\textbar{}S) = 0 is met by a reactive chemotaxis map, which supports Clark rather than a needed search plan. [N2 self-refit-preferences] Same mechanism as our MM loop, mixed-sign objective: roughly E[c] + H \ensuremath{-} I, because the EFE novelty term is kept. Sajid 2021 Sec. 5 shows the local optima: agents learn to prefer absorbing holes. [N3 dark-room] Precondition or limitation that applies to us in full. Our answer is a persistent disturbance plus a tilted reference measure. Turner's anti-POWER reading (N16) is the formal statement. Klein 2016 Sec. 4 adds that Conant--Ashby good states are states of the regulated system, not the regulator, and that error-controlled regulation is not GRT-optimal, which backs our split of Z from S and the two-channel picture. Sun--Firestone 2020 is a forum piece: the self-prediction reply is false or trivial. On the AIF side, Champion 2024 (N10) and Ueltzhoffer 2018 are the empirical collapses.

\textbf{Idea.} To persist, an agent keeps its sensory states in a small set by minimising their entropy, which under ergodicity equals time-average surprisal, bounded by variational free energy.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N1a fep-self-entropy/particular-physics [=]: Non-equilibrium steady state with Markov blankets; self-entropy H(P) = H(P\textbar{}E) + I(E,P); EFE as the Lagrangian of autonomous paths with the particle's own marginal as preference.
\item Absorbed N1b fep-self-entropy/critiques [L]: The derivation fails in places: false free-energy lemma, inequivalent blankets, narrow linear conditions, ergodicity failure.
\item Absorbed N2 self-refit-preferences [\ensuremath{\approx}]: The preference density is refit to the agent's own outcomes and then used as the target: Pepper's across-episode Dirichlet refit, the self-prior density on replay, preferences learned from a world model with attention over memories.
\item Absorbed N3 dark-room [L]: A surprise minimiser should seek a dark room; replies are a cross-entropy against an evolved model, epistemic value, or optimistic priors.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Friston-free12b}: States our objective a*=argmin H(X\textbar{}m) (Eq.7) then optimises pointwise surprise under a frozen density (Eq.9): one MM step without refit
\item \citet{Friston-free12c}: Def 1 Eq 7: argmin over a deterministic internal map of H(S\textbar{}m), our objective with Z=S, determinism assumed; IB form S\_B*=H(S)+H(R\textbar{}S) bounds ours with gap zero iff H(R\textbar{}S)=0
\item \citet{Friston-free19}: FEP self-entropy H(P)=H(P\textbar{}E)+I(E,P); text says minimising it minimises the mutual information: our sign, from the FEP itself
\item \citet{Sajid-exploration21}: Pepper: across-episode Dirichlet refit of preferences on own outcomes; concentration in static worlds, absorbing optima; our MM loop with mixed sign
\item \citet{Kim-emergence25}: Self-prior density refit on replay used as preference: closest implementation of our Gibbs surrogate, but keeps an opposite-sign novelty term
\end{itemize}

\subsection{N4 setpoint-surrogates}\label{node:S3-N4-setpoint-surrogates}
\textit{Merged from} N4a setpoint-surrogates/interoceptive-allostatic (20); N4b setpoint-surrogates/reflexive-vfe-control (5). \textbf{Relation} $\subset$: Special case. These papers claim H(S\textbar{}m), the spread, Friston 2010 Eq. 3, but with m fixed they control the cross-entropy H(p) + KL(p\ensuremath{\|}p\_m): E[c] with c quadratic, our tilted case, equivalently the MM inner step with q frozen at a set-point density.

\textbf{Idea.} The regulated density is fixed at a set point held as a prior mean; action descends VFE or EFE against it.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N4a setpoint-surrogates/interoceptive-allostatic [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Homeostasis as interoceptive prediction error, allostasis as predictive shifting of priors.
\item Absorbed N4b setpoint-surrogates/reflexive-vfe-control [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Instantaneous VFE descent recovers PID, LQG/Kalman--Bucy and the Watt governor.
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{Group B. Flipped sign on the marginal: the expected-free-energy family}

\subsection{N5 efe-decompositions}\label{node:S3-N5-efe-decompositions}
\textit{Merged from} N7 epistemic-term-as-value-of-information (10); N8 efe-on-occupancy (2); N9 reward-as-preference (8). \textbf{Relation} $-$/\textsf{L}/$\approx$: Opposite sign on H(Z) and on I(Z;E); same sign on the conditional H(Z\textbar{}E), the ambiguity penalty, which matches our H(Z\textbar{}S,A). Friston 2016 Eq. 6 states it outright: with flat preferences G = \ensuremath{-}H(Z) + H(Z\textbar{}S) + const. Champion 2026 shows the root form recovers all four EFE forms only if C\_o = A C\_s (Eqs. 7 to 9). [N7 epistemic-term-as-value-of-information] Precondition or limitation of EFE, tool for us: our posterior-as-state makes the closed-loop belief MDP explicit, so no bonus is needed, and our entropy term never cancels. Markovic 2021: one-step EFE is not asymptotically efficient in stationary bandits for any fixed \ensuremath{\lambda}, and wins in switching bandits. Champion 2021: under a fully factorised posterior the outcome term drops out and the agent only seeks unambiguous states, an outcome-blind certainty seeker whose remaining term has our sign on H(Z\textbar{}S,A). Kouw 2026 recasts the drive as a constraint, information \ensuremath{\geq} goal-prior entropy, with inactive, interior and saturated regimes. [N8 efe-on-occupancy] Opposite sign, same polytope. Ours is \ensuremath{\langle}\ensuremath{\ell},q\ensuremath{\rangle} + H(q), concave, vertex optima; our MM reward is log \ensuremath{\tilde{p}} + log q. The sharpest positioning node. [N9 reward-as-preference] Same mechanism, different objective: mirrors our reward-channel theorem with the opposite tie-break. DaCosta 2024 has no \ensuremath{\beta}-limit theorem; it supplies the universality claim and a precise-agent premise, h\textbar{}s deterministic, Def. 2.1, Lemma D.1, under which EFE is exact. Friston 2012 (Eq. 9) reproduces \ensuremath{\pi}* exactly by a prior whose transitions are the optimal policy's, a complete-class argument.

\textbf{Idea.} EFE = extrinsic + epistemic value = risk + ambiguity; KL control as the observed case; novelty and sophisticated extensions.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N7 epistemic-term-as-value-of-information [L]: What the epistemic term does: constant in linear-Gaussian models, a value-of-information surrogate in an open-loop belief MDP, a \ensuremath{\rho}-POMDP belief utility, inference on augmented models, an exploration bonus in bandits, a constraint with a KKT multiplier.
\item Absorbed N8 efe-on-occupancy [\ensuremath{-}]: EFE written on the state-action occupancy is \ensuremath{\langle}\ensuremath{\ell},q\ensuremath{\rangle} \ensuremath{-} H(q), a convex MDP with local reward log \ensuremath{\tilde{p}} \ensuremath{-} log q; time-averaged AIF is average-cost control against a fixed reference with an entropy rate.
\item Absorbed N9 reward-as-preference [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Preferences C \ensuremath{\propto} exp(\ensuremath{\beta}R); as \ensuremath{\beta} \ensuremath{\to} \ensuremath{\infty} sophisticated EFE is backward induction, with ties broken to maximum future entropy, DaCosta 2023.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Friston-active15}: Canonical EFE = extrinsic + epistemic value; the epistemic MI bonus is the sign flip we contrast against
\item \citet{Milosevic-active26}: EFE on the occupancy polytope is \textless{}l,q\textgreater{}-H(q), a convex MDP with local reward log p\ensuremath{\sim} - log q: exact sign mirror of our \textless{}l,q\textgreater{}+H(q) and MM reward
\item \citet{DaCosta-reward23}: Reward recovered as beta-\textgreater{}inf limit of EFE with ties broken to maximum entropy: mirror of our reward-channel theorem with opposite tie-break
\end{itemize}

\subsection{N10 deep-aif-tools}\label{node:S3-N10-deep-aif-tools}
\textit{Merged from} N6 objective-axes-and-taxonomies (12). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: Tool we use: the implementations our MM actor-critic parallels. Precondition or limitation on the evidence: Ueltzhoffer 2018 reports dark-room collapse without a hard-wired set point, and Champion 2024's EFE critic (Eq. 18) collapses to one deterministic action that keeps the transition-encoder KL small and earns zero reward; DAI\_MC was not replicated on Animal-AI. [N6 objective-axes-and-taxonomies] Tool we use for positioning. Ours is one step past evidence: E[c] + H. Biehl 2018 finds no catalogued motivation with our sign. Millidge's free energy of the future penalises information gain and so sits on our side. L\'azaro-Gredilla 2024 shows marginal, MAP, marginal-MAP inference and planning differ only in the weights on the entropy terms. Gottwald--Braun 2020 Sec. 5.3: Q(a) (Eq. 26) depends on q, so a reference that moves during optimisation leaves the objective undefined; our refit escapes this because each ln \ensuremath{\hat{q}} majorises one fixed concave H. - \textbf{Note, new idea, not a node.} Csaky 2026 proves perfect prediction of a target comes either from identifying a target-relevant quotient or from overwrite control that makes the target action-determined (Prop. 1(iii), Thm. 8), with capacity log\textbar{}supp V\textbar{} for overwrite (Cor. 2). Overwrite is our regulator; the paper penalises it through the posterior gap H(Q\textbar{}T). Its regret-transfer oscillation \ensuremath{\Omega} (Thm. 2) has no home node. Cross-link to S6/A.

\textbf{Idea.} Neural AIF: amortised EFE critics, MCTS, world models, evolution strategies.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N6 objective-axes-and-taxonomies [T]: Frameworks placing all probabilistic objectives on one axis: divergence E[c] \ensuremath{-} H versus evidence E[c]; posterior functionals M(d, \^a); action-perception divergence; constrained max-entropy; weightings of the entropy terms of one variational problem; alternative functionals, generalised free energy, free energy of the future.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Biehl-expanding18}: Posterior-functional catalogue: none of EFE, empowerment, predictive information, knowledge seeking has our sign; deterministic argmax default
\item \citet{Millidge-understanding21}: Orders objectives divergence E[c]-H vs evidence E[c]; ours is the next step E[c]+H
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{Group C. The middle term I(A;Z\textbar{}S), maximised}

\subsection{N11 empowerment-capacity}\label{node:S3-N11-empowerment-capacity}
\textit{Merged from} N11a empowerment-capacity/stabilisation (3); N12 variational-empowerment (18); N15 empowerment-of-others (13); N16 path-entropy-and-power (1). \textbf{Relation} $-$/$\approx$: Opposite sign on a neighbouring quantity. C(s) \ensuremath{\geq} I(A;Z\textbar{}S=s); we drive the realised term to zero and never touch the capacity: leverage kept, not spent randomly. Agreement only on noise: state-dependent noise lowers empowerment and raises our H(Z\textbar{}S,A). In deterministic worlds empowerment is log-reachability, which we drive down. Every paper needs the channel. Blahut 1972 is also a tool for the algorithm: Thm. 4, R(D) = sD + min\_q min\_Q F with q refit to the output marginal, is the template of our MM step, H(Z) = min\_\ensuremath{\hat{q}} E[\ensuremath{-}ln \ensuremath{\hat{q}}(Z)] with \ensuremath{\hat{q}} refit to the induced marginal. Tiomkin 2025's feedback-aware process empowerment is a closed-loop variant that sits closer to the realised term of N13. Grasso 2022 uses empowerment as a secondary objective for shape maintenance in neural cellular automata, a regulation-plus-empowerment example. [N11a empowerment-capacity/stabilisation] same behaviour, different mechanism: a regulator seeks low disturbance gain on Z. \textbf{Keys.} \citet{Zhao-efficient20}, \citet{Tiomkin-intrinsic22}, \citet{Jung-empowerment11}, \citet{CatenacciVolpi-skilled23}. \textbf{Saturation.} Citers of \citet{Zhao-efficient20} (14) and \citet{Tiomkin-intrinsic22} (19). [N12 variational-empowerment] Opposite sign. Leibfried 2019 Eq. 5 accumulates I(A;S'\textbar{}S) at \ensuremath{\pi}, exactly our middle term maximised, and forces stochastic optima for \ensuremath{\beta} \textgreater{} 0. Cao 2025 maximises a capacity gap, max\_\ensuremath{\pi} I(s';a\textbar{}M) under a causal model minus under a dense one (Eqs. 3, 7). Bastankhah 2026 maximises skill empowerment over the discounted occupancy, I(Z;S\textsuperscript{+}\textbar{}s\_0); its optimum puts mass only on occupancy-polytope vertices, Lemma B.1, B.2, the same vertex mechanism as ours with the opposite sign, and with policy minimality, argmin I(S;A) within an occupancy class (Def. 5.5), the learned representations ignore uncontrollable, control-irrelevant factors (Thm. 5.4, Cor. 5.6), a near match to action sufficiency. Cross-list with S6 learned outcome spaces. [N15 empowerment-of-others] Opposite sign on the other agent's middle term. \citet{Myers-learning24}, the assistance paper, not the contrastive-successor-features paper under the same key, evaluates it at the human's behaviour policy, so a deterministic human has zero effective empowerment. Selmonaj 2025 gives a Shapley-style attribution of each agent's causal effect on teammates' empowerment in MARL. [N16 path-entropy-and-power] Opposite sign on two or three terms. MOP maximises H(A\textbar{}S) + H(S'\textbar{}S,A). Ay 2008 Eq. 22: predictive information is H(X\_\{t+1\}) minus a policy-independent noise term, so maximising it maximises the outcome marginal. POWER is the support function of the occupancy polytope; our objective is anti-POWER-seeking, collapsing onto one recurrent class, the formal dark room.

\textbf{Idea.} Empowerment is the capacity C(s) = max\_p I(A\^{}n; S\_\{t+n\} \textbar{} s) of the action-to-future-sensor channel, a task-free "keep your options open" drive; Blahut--Arimoto computes it.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N11a empowerment-capacity/stabilisation [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Empowerment maximisers balance pendula because capacity peaks at the unstable high-gain upright state; the landscape inverts with more actuator power.
\item Absorbed N12 variational-empowerment [\ensuremath{-}]: Barber--Agakov or contrastive bounds turn empowerment into a deep-RL intrinsic reward.
\item Absorbed N15 empowerment-of-others [\ensuremath{-}]: Maximise another agent's empowerment as a proxy for assistance, or minimise it for antagonism; attribute teammates' empowerment to agents.
\item Absorbed N16 path-entropy-and-power [\ensuremath{-}]: Keep options open without an explicit channel: POWER, the maximum occupancy principle, causal entropic forces, predictive information.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Blahut-computation72}: Capacity solver for empowerment, Thms 1, 3, and, via Thm 4, refit q to the output marginal, the template of our MM step
\item \citet{Klyubin-empowerment05}: Founding empowerment C(s)=max\_p I(A\^{}n;S\_\{t+n\}\textbar{}s): a capacity that upper-bounds our middle term, maximised
\item \citet{Bastankhah-learning26}: Skill empowerment over discounted occupancy: mass only on occupancy-polytope vertices, Lemma B.1/B.2; invariance to control-irrelevant factors, Thm 5.4, Cor 5.6; policy minimality min I(S;A), Def 5.5,
\item \citet{Turner-optimal19}: POWER is the support function of the occupancy polytope; our objective is anti-POWER, collapse to a 1-cycle: the formal dark room
\end{itemize}

\subsection{N13 realised-action-outcome-information}\label{node:S3-N13-realised-action-outcome-information}
\textit{Merged from} N13a realised-action-outcome-information/flow-measures (10). \textbf{Relation} $-$/\textsf{T}: Literally our middle term; opposite sign when used as a bonus, the same functional when used as a diagnostic. Causal action influence separates "no leverage" (C(s)=0) from "leverage spent deterministically". Bonuses: Massari 2021's loss gap of two VIB predictors approximates \ensuremath{\alpha} I(Z;A\_t\textbar{}S\_t) at the current policy, never maximised over p(A); Xu 2025 maximises W(p(h\textbar{}a), p(h)); Grasso 2023 uses plug-in I(A;S\_\{t+k\}) with no max. Diagnostics: Song 2025 estimates the agent's own I(a\_t;s*\textbar{}s\_t) at its policy with two InfoNCE critics; Furuta 2021's policy information capacity I(\ensuremath{\theta}; return) grades task difficulty. ELDEN (Hu 2023) optimises no mutual information: its bonus is the ensemble variance of a Jacobian-thresholded dependency graph (Eq. 1), uncertainty about whether leverage exists. [N13a realised-action-outcome-information/flow-measures] tool we use. Ay--Polani flow I(A\ensuremath{\to}Z\textbar{}\^{}S) averaged over the occupancy is exactly I(A;Z\textbar{}S) because A's only parent is S, so the middle term is a causal flow. Janzing 2013 Thm. 4 splits causal strength as C\_\{A\ensuremath{\to}Z\} = I(A;Z\textbar{}S) + D[P(Z\textbar{}S) \ensuremath{\|} \ensuremath{\Sigma}\_a P(Z\textbar{}S,a)P(a)]; at our deterministic optimum A = f(S) the first term is zero and the second is the whole, a measure of leverage kept and spent deterministically, note on this node, not a new node. Janzing Sec. 3 also shows the Ay--Polani flow, transfer entropy and directed information violate its postulates on small DAGs. Massey 1990: I(X\^{}N\ensuremath{\to}Y\^{}N) \ensuremath{\leq} I(X\^{}N;Y\^{}N) with equality without feedback (Thm. 1). Klyubin 2007 is not a flow measure but a perception-action infomax: evolved deterministic finite-state controllers maximise I(R\_0;M\_t) (Sec. 4.4) and come out near-deterministic with near-hard partitions. Krakauer 2020 defines individuality as aggregates that propagate information from past to future. \textbf{Keys.} \citet{Ay-information08}, \citet{Janzing-quantifying13}, \citet{Massey-causality90}, \citet{Schreiber-measuring00}, \citet{Touchette-information00}, \citet{Klyubin-organization04}, \citet{Klyubin-representations07}, \citet{Krakauer-information20}. \textbf{Saturation.} 58 matching titles in the 3,509 pool; the rest are applications. \citet{Bertschinger-autonomy08} unread.

\textbf{Idea.} I(A;Z\textbar{}S=s) evaluated at a given action law, used as a controllability detector, an evaluation diagnostic or a bonus.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed N13a realised-action-outcome-information/flow-measures [T]: Directed information, transfer entropy, causal information flow by intervention, causal strength, autonomy, individuality.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
