\subsection{B3. anti-collapse-as-reference-measure}\label{node:S6-S6-B-anti-collapse-as-reference-measure}
\textit{Merged from} S6/B/information-bottleneck (14). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: Tool we use. The distribution on which the regulariser is enforced is \ensuremath{\mu}. Enforced on the policy's occupancy, H(Z) is pinned; enforced on exploratory data, the policy can concentrate without the encoder collapsing. \citet{Pasand-stable26} gives a reason to fix \ensuremath{\mu} = N(0,I) in the MM loop: isotropic Gaussian embeddings are maximum-entropy for a fixed variance budget and stay stable under non-stationary targets, and the refit \ensuremath{\hat{q}}\_k is such a target. [S6/B/information-bottleneck] Tool we use. Achille's separation principle, predict the regulated measurement, is the sensor-outcome fallback. A learned prior collapses whenever the task term allows it. The bottleneck is on the representation's I(S;Z\_rep), not on the outcome term I(S;Z) of our identity.

\textbf{Idea.} Every non-action anti-collapse regulariser is an entropy, or an entropy proxy, of the latent marginal against a declared reference: uniform on the sphere, N(0,I) in SIGReg, Gaussian potentials in CRAR, second moments in VICReg.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S6/B/information-bottleneck [T]: Minimal sufficient representations through a \ensuremath{\beta} I(X;Z) penalty against a reference: a fixed Gaussian, a learned prior, a backward encoder, or a noise channel.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Wang-understanding20}: Uniformity loss is a KDE entropy against uniform on the sphere; anchors the anti-collapse-is-a-reference-measure reading
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{C. The disturbance: drop, split, estimate, keep, or fight, 97 records,}

\subsection{C2. controllable-uncontrollable-split}\label{node:S6-S6-C-controllable-uncontrollable-split}
\textit{Merged from} S6/C/exogenous-filtering (14). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}/\textsf{L}: Tool we use. Represent D, do not control it. Every learned rule found keeps ancestors of controllable variables, CDL, or of the reward (Huang, FANS-RL), and every detector sees only A \ensuremath{\to} S\ensuremath{'} edges. [S6/C/exogenous-filtering] Precondition or limitation, and a stance we reject. Dietterich's Theorem 1 needs R = R\_exo(x) + R\_end(e,x,a). With Z = (Z\_x, Z\_e), H(Z) = H(Z\_x) + H(Z\_e \textbar{} Z\_x), and the second term depends on how the policy couples Z\_e to the disturbance, which is the error-controlled regulator's whole job.

\textbf{Idea.} Keep the action-free factors in their own block, by adversarial split, independently controllable factors, state-baseline plus action-advantage channels, or separate action and world effects, or keep the ancestors of a target in a causal graph. Also covers per-state detectors of causal action influence.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S6/C/exogenous-filtering [L]: Factor the state into endogenous and exogenous parts and drop the exogenous one, Exo/Endo MDP, Denoised MDP, IFactor, distractor-robust world models.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Kooi-disentangled23}: Keeps uncontrollable factors in a separate block; the representational template for represent-but-do-not-control
\item \citet{Pendharkar-predictive26}: Direct evidence that inverse dynamics and JEPA objectives discard exogenous control-relevant features; reward grounding restores them
\item \citet{Dietterich-discovering18}: Exogenous removal preserves optimality only for additive rewards, Thm 1; H(Z) is not additive, so filtering fails for a regulator
\end{itemize}

\subsection{C3. disturbance-estimation}\label{node:S6-S6-C-disturbance-estimation}
\textit{Merged from} S6/C/exo-mdp-keep-the-input (11); S6/C/adversarial-disturbance (8). \textbf{Relation} $\approx$/$\subset$/$-$: Same mechanism, different objective. These are Conant and Ashby's cause-controlled and error-controlled channels realised in learning, and the DOB residual is the complement of the note's inverse loss. [S6/C/exo-mdp-keep-the-input] Special case, a persistent-disturbance class. Sinclair says filtering gives "demand-agnostic policies, which are highly sub-optimal". Wan shows every MDP is an Exo-MDP and observing D is worth a \ensuremath{\surd}r regret factor. [S6/C/adversarial-disturbance] Opposite sign on the disturbance law. Entropy regulation needs the natural law of D, so adversarial training distorts the outcome law that \ensuremath{\hat{q}} fits.

\textbf{Idea.} Recover the disturbance from the transition residual the action does not explain, DOB, ADRC, NeuralIMC, infer each action's current impact from history under drift \citep{Zeng-moving23}, or distil a privileged teacher that sees the disturbance into a history-based student, RMA, Neural-Fly.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S6/C/exo-mdp-keep-the-input [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Keep the exogenous input in the state and exploit its action-independent law, hindsight planning, input-dependent baselines, Exo-MDP regret.
\item Absorbed S6/C/adversarial-disturbance [\ensuremath{-}]: The disturbance is a learned worst-case adversary (RARL, H\ensuremath{\infty} RL).
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Kumar-rma21}: Privileged teacher distilled into history student: the cause-controlled and error-controlled channels realised in deep RL
\end{itemize}

\subsection{D. internal-model-principle-in-learning}\label{node:S6-S6-D-internal-model-principle-in-learning}
\textbf{Relation} $=$: Same objective in the H(Z) \ensuremath{\to} 0 limit. It gives the precise sense of "represent the disturbance": a memory driven by the regulated error. Signal detection is the condition that excludes the constant-outcome solution.

\textbf{Idea.} An error-driven regulator that achieves y \ensuremath{\to} 0 against a persistent class must contain a generator of that class, Sontag. In learning: ADP or RL output regulation, which either measures the exosignal or copies a known spectrum; adaptive or identification-based internal models, GP internal models and FANS-RL, which learn a generator.

\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Sontag-adaptation03}: IMP with signal detection: an error-driven regulator must carry a generator of the disturbance class; defines represent-not-observe
\item \citet{Qasem-adaptive22}: Refutes the session-1 claim that RL/ADP never learns the exosystem; learns unknown oscillator frequencies, but the exosignal is measured
\item \citet{Zhao-incremental24}: promoted: W4 full read: d and v never measured, cancelled by a given minimal polynomial; anchors the D gap sentence in the survey
\end{itemize}
\memb{Family S6.F19} adaptive optimal output regulation by ADP, 21 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Qasem-adaptive22}; \citealp{Zhao-incremental24}; \citealp{Gao-adaptive16}; \citealp{Chen-robust21}; \citealp{Rizvi-reinforcement22}; \citealp{Qasem-autonomous23}; \citealp{Jing-incremental25}; \citealp{Gao-learningbased18}; \citealp{Liu-adaptive18}; \citealp{Jiang-learningbased20}; \citealp{Gao-learningbased22}; \citealp{Gao-reinforcement22}; \citealp{Xie-optimal22}; \citealp{Alsalti-efficient23}; \citealp{Amirparast-undiscounted23}; \citealp{Cotta-control23}; \citealp{Jiang-adpbased23}; \citealp{Gao-resilient24}; \citealp{Wang-adaptive24}; \citealp{Lin-datadriven25b}; \citealp{Lin-direct25}}}

\subsection{E. learned-outcome-spaces}\label{node:S6-S6-E-learned-outcome-spaces}
\textbf{Relation} $\approx$: Same mechanism, different objective. Only IC2 shares our sign; Skew-Fit and PMA push conditional entropy down and marginal entropy up, and we keep only the first half. The recurring anti-gaming device is decoupling: the regulator's reward never trains the encoder.

\textbf{Idea.} Intrinsic or goal objectives computed on a latent outcome whose encoder is fixed (RE3), trained by an independent loss (APT, IC2), learned from passive data and frozen, IMGEP, Skew-Fit, learned reward-free as a goal-progress embedding \citep{Yu-unsupervised19}, or grounded in measurements, DFP.

\section{S7 Multiple regulators}
375 papers in 12 nodes: 17 core, 65 support, 293 background; 19 families hold 223 papers.

\medskip\textsc{1. S7/sign: which way the coupling points}

\subsection{1.1 S7/sign/crowd-averse}\label{node:S7-S7-sign-crowd-averse}
\textbf{Relation} $-$: opposite sign. Geist's CURL/MaxEnt reward -ln mu is this class's entropy-maximising member; ours is ln mu. \citet{Bakaryan-regularity23} solves the stationary MFG with exactly logarithmic coupling by a Hopf-Cole reduction to one scalar elliptic equation; the reduction uses only the log form and may transfer to our sign.

\textbf{Idea.} Monotone, Lasry-Lions, MFGs and congestion, minority and Wardrop games: cost rises with local density, which gives uniqueness and stability.

\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Lasry-mean07}: founding Lasry-Lions monotonicity; our log coupling reverses the inequality (sum of two KLs \textgreater{}= 0), so uniqueness fails
\end{itemize}
\memb{Family S7.F16} minority and majority games, 4 papers, 3 here: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Arthur-inductive94}; \citealp{Challet-emergence97}; \citealp{Marsili-exact00}}}

\subsection{1.2 S7/sign/crowd-seeking}\label{node:S7-S7-sign-crowd-seeking}
\textit{Merged from} S7/sign/segregation (8); S7/niches/crowd-dynamics (14). \textbf{Relation} $=$/\textsf{L}/$\approx$: same objective, continuous-state form. With f = -ln m the energy of \citet{Cesaroni-concentration19} is control cost + E[V] + H(m). Log coupling is subcritical, so there is existence without blow-up and without uniqueness at small noise. [S7/sign/segregation] precondition. This is the formal model of "regulators partition into niches". It shows that partition needs a cross-population crowd-averse term, which our objective lacks on its own. [S7/niches/crowd-dynamics] same mechanism, different objective. Crowd-seeking without congestion gives one crowd, or several clusters when the reach is local. Hegselmann's epsilon is the analogue of an action's reach on the shared outcome channel. Meylahn shows that for learning agents ergodicity depends on the learning rule.

\textbf{Idea.} Focusing, aggregating MFGs and the majority game: cost falls with density. Equilibria exist under growth conditions but are not unique once noise is small. Strong aggregation blows up. Mass concentrates as noise vanishes, and stationary states of the majority game sit at hypercube corners. Individual noise above a threshold set by the aggregation rate restores well-posedness for mildly non-monotone couplings \citep{Cirant-long21}, so non-uniqueness is a small-noise statement. Binary majority MFGs with own-type stubbornness have several MFG equilibria where the N-player game has one \citep{DaiPra-polarization21}. Kuramoto synchronisation is an aggregating MFG with a bifurcation from incoherence \citep{Yin-synchronization12}.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S7/sign/segregation [L]: Several populations, each attracted to its own kind or repelled by the others.
\item Absorbed S7/niches/crowd-dynamics [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Attraction-driven dynamics: consensus, DeGroot, Vicsek, Couzin, Cucker-Smale flocking as an MFG with a mean-velocity alignment cost, bounded confidence, which fragments into clusters when attraction is local, Schelling segregation from mild own-type preference, and Hotelling/Krugman agglomeration versus dispersion.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Cesaroni-concentration19}: focusing MFG energy with log coupling is control cost + E[V] + H(m): our objective in continuous state; mass concentrates as noise vanishes
\item \citet{Cirant-bifurcation17}: explicit noise threshold nu* for segregated vs mixed equilibria; template for the niche prediction
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{2. S7/learn: convergence and selection among many equilibria}

\subsection{2.2 S7/learn/potential}\label{node:S7-S7-learn-potential}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: tool, with a limitation. A shared outcome makes -H a potential. MPG theory assumes linear rewards, so it covers each MM step with ln q frozen, not the outer refit. FP converges without selecting a vertex; multiplicative weights select a pure equilibrium generically but not a particular one, which is the dynamic analogue of the vertex argument.

\textbf{Idea.} Potential and Markov potential games.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Identical-interest games have the FP property, with U as Lyapunov function, \citet{MondererShapley-fictitious96} Thm A. In the 2x2 pure coordination game FP can converge to the purely mixed NE, Remark 2, and convergence to pure NE is only conjectured for generic games.
\item Hedge and replicator dynamics in congestion games have the expected potential as Lyapunov function, \citet{Kleinberg-multiplicative09} Thm 3.2, and Tr J = 0 leaves only weakly stable NE as stable points, which are generically pure, Thm 4.1.
\item BR dynamics converge generically and exponentially to \textbf{pure} NE; MPGs have deterministic Nash policies, and independent PG reaches stationary points of the potential.
\item Satisficing independent learners reach approximate equilibria in weakly acyclic games, a superclass of potential games (\citet{Yongacoglu-satisficing23}, 24).
\item Regret matching reaches the correlated-equilibrium set in every finite game, with no potential \citep{Hart-simple00}.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Leonardos-global21}: MPGs have deterministic Nash policies and independent PG converges; covers only the inner MM step, linear reward,
\end{itemize}
\memb{Family S7.F7} independent policy gradient in Markov potential games, 12 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Leonardos-global21}; \citealp{Macua-learning18}; \citealp{Cen-independent22}; \citealp{Chen-convergence22}; \citealp{Ding-independent22}; \citealp{Fox-independent21}; \citealp{Maheshwari-independent22}; \citealp{Zhang-global22}; \citealp{Sun-provably23}; \citealp{Alatur-independent24}; \citealp{Cheng-provable24}; \citealp{Jordan-independent24}}}

\subsection{2.3 S7/learn/general-utility}\label{node:S7-S7-learn-general-utility}
\textbf{Relation} $\supset$: generalisation with the opposite curvature. \citet{Gemp-convex24} gets pure NE when utilities are \textbf{concave} in own occupancy. Ours, -H, are convex, so best responses are disconnected vertex sets and only mixed NE over deterministic policies are guaranteed. \citet{Xia-team25} gets its pure stationary equilibrium from the same structure as ours: Var = min\_y E(r - y)\^{}2 is a minimum of occupancy-linear costs, just as H = min\_q E[-ln q]. Its Algorithm 1 sets y to the team mean and then solves per-player average-cost MDPs, which is our MM loop with y in place of q-hat; Thm 2 gives a pure stationary NE and Thm 5 finite convergence to a local minimum. It is a team objective, not own entropies. \citet{Barakat-convex26} equates NE with first-order stationary points by agent-wise gradient domination in coupled general-utility Markov games; whether it needs concave utilities is unchecked.

\textbf{Idea.} n-player games where each utility is a function of the player's own occupancy: convex Markov games, multi-agent state entropy, stable and population games, team variance games, and general-utility Markov games with coupled occupancies.

\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Gemp-convex24}: pure NE need utilities concave in own occupancy; ours are convex, so only mixed NE over deterministic policies are guaranteed
\item \citet{Xia-team25}: promoted: Var = min\_y E(r-y)\^{}2 is a min of occupancy-linear costs like H = min\_q E[-ln q]; Alg 1 is our MM loop per player, pure stationary NE, Thm 2,
\end{itemize}

\subsection{2.4 S7/learn/noise-selection}\label{node:S7-S7-learn-noise-selection}
\textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: tool, with a limitation. This is vertex selection for concave minimisation. Waiting times grow exponentially in the basin depth as noise goes to 0. Two qualifications: on a d = 1 lattice Blume's Fact 11 gives a convergence rate independent of population size, and \citet{Borowski-fast17} gives polynomial mixing for semi-anonymous potential games. Payoff-based selection of the welfare optimum now has its own node, 2.6.

\textbf{Idea.} In finite games, vanishing noise picks one equilibrium, and \textbf{the noise model decides which}.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Mutation-driven best reply, KMR, Young, picks the largest basin, i.e. risk dominance.
\item Log-linear, Gibbs, revision picks the risk-dominant equilibrium in 2x2 games, which is the potential maximiser. For n x n games the Gibbs stationary state needs a Monderer-Shapley potential, \citet{Blume-statistical93} Eq 6.2, Def 6.3, and Thm 6.4 selects without one. Logit-response selects potential maximisers only in exact potential games, and the answer depends on the revision protocol \citep{AlosFerrer-logit10}.
\item Log-linear and Metropolis learning share one stochastically stable set but take different transient paths \citep{Jaleel-path18}, so the noise model sets the path as well as the limit.
\item Risk attitude also selects: an entropic risk measure on opponent-induced payoff uncertainty moves the equilibrium that mutation and logit dynamics pick \citep{Mohammadi-entropic26}.
\item Independent deep learners in stag hunts land on the risk-dominant equilibrium, and prosocial weights enlarge the payoff-dominant basin. Sequential social dilemmas and the evolutionary stag hunt are the same question \citep{Leibo-multi17,Bulbulia-spreading11}.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Kandori-learning93}: founding stochastic stability: noisy best reply selects the largest basin, risk dominance, with exponential waiting time
\item \citet{Zhang-equilibrium24}: log-linear learning selects the potential maximiser, lifted to Markov potential games; restates Blume and Young
\end{itemize}
\memb{Family S7.F1} stochastic stability under vanishing noise, 29 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Kandori-learning93}; \citealp{Foster-stochastic90}; \citealp{Blume-statistical93}; \citealp{Young-evolution93}; \citealp{Marden-revisiting12}; \citealp{Borowski-fast17}; \citealp{Ellison-learning93}\dag; \citealp{Blume-statistical95}; \citealp{Kandori-evolution95}; \citealp{Bergin-evolution96}; \citealp{Rhode-comment96}\dag; \citealp{Robson-efficient96}\dag; \citealp{Binmore-muddling97}; \citealp{Young-individual98}\dag; \citealp{Ellison-basins00}\dag; \citealp{Anderson-minimum01}; \citealp{Miekisz-stochastic04}; \citealp{AlosFerrer-logit10}; \citealp{Okada-loglinear12}; \citealp{Chasparis-stochastic17}; \citealp{Lahkar-equilibrium17}\dag; \citealp{Tatarenko-logit17}; \citealp{Hasanbeig-game18}; \citealp{Jaleel-path18}; \citealp{NaleczJawecki-mean18}; \citealp{Tatarenko-independent18}; \citealp{Staudigl-large22}; \citealp{Mukhopadhyay-analogies25}; \citealp{Mohammadi-entropic26}}}

\subsection{2.5 S7/learn/mfg-selection}\label{node:S7-S7-learn-mfg-selection}
\textit{Merged from} S7/learn/mfg-algorithms (17). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}: tool, same objective class. Symmetric regulators break symmetry at random into concentrated configurations, and shared noise both stabilises the loop and selects the potential optimum. [S7/learn/mfg-algorithms] tool we use. Freeze q and best-respond is an MFG best response; refitting q is the mean-field update. FP averaging and OMD are the stabilisers our MM loop needs.

\textbf{Idea.} Selection and learning in non-monotone MFGs.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item In a two-state MFG with a non-monotone cost only the entropy solution is the N-player limit \citep{Bayraktar-non19}, the finite-state predecessor of \citet{Cecchin-selection21}.
\item Finite-N and zero-common-noise limits pick each extreme equilibrium with probability 1/2, while the minimal-cost criterion picks the middle one.
\item Vanishing Wright-Fisher common noise picks the potential, MFC, minimiser.
\item Common noise used as exploration makes tilted FP converge, while plain FP oscillates.
\item Herding MFGs have a finite-time learning guarantee.
\item In potential MFGs, restrictions of potential minimisers to [t1, T] are stable, \citet{Briani-stable17} Thm 4.3, stable solutions are local attractors of fictitious play, Thm 5.1, and symmetry breaking gives multiplicity, Prop 3.3.
\item MF-OMO recasts the discrete-time MFG equilibrium as a bounded optimisation over occupation measures that can retrieve all NE without monotonicity or contraction \citep{Guo-mfomo22}, a route to enumerating the vertex equilibria.
\item Absorbed S7/learn/mfg-algorithms [T]: Fictitious play, online mirror descent, occupation-measure solvers and deep variants for MFGs, with exploitability as the metric.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Delarue-exploration21}: common noise makes fictitious play converge without monotonicity; plain FP oscillates; design lever for the MM loop
\item \citet{Cecchin-selection21}: vanishing common noise selects the potential, MFC, minimiser in finite-state potential MFGs; two-state majority example is our game
\item \citet{Zeng-learning24}: only finite-time learning guarantee for a crowd-seeking, herding, MFG; r = q mu\^{}p, ln mu is its p-\textgreater{}0 member
\end{itemize}

\subsection{2.6 S7/learn/team-optimum}\label{node:S7-S7-learn-team-optimum}
\textit{Merged from} S7/learn/goore-game (6). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{T}/$\approx$: tool, and the stateless solution of our selection question. Take one virtual node per letter, f\_z(a) = K(z\textbar{}a) and U\_z(x) = x ln x. Then sum\_z U\_z(rbar\_z) = -H(Z), which is convex in p, so the maximiser is a pure profile, K = 1 suffices, and G-NUM reduces to \citet{Marden-achieving14}, Remark 3. What is missing is state: payoffs are deterministic and stateless, and the outcome letters must be mapped onto acting nodes that satisfy interdependence, which no paper does. [S7/learn/goore-game] same setting, different objective. It is a team game with a shared outcome and a set-point, the learning-theoretic twin of Daisyworld; \citet{Andrew-goore86} pairs the two. The agents aim at a shared reward peak, not at the outcome's spread, so regulation is to a set-point and not to a low-entropy law.

\textbf{Idea.} Payoff-based rules whose stochastically stable states maximise welfare sum\_i U\_i, with no potential needed.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Content/discontent learning \citep{Marden-achieving14} and trial-and-error learning \citep{PradelskiYoung-learning12} select the welfare-maximising pure profile.
\item Aspiration learning, efficient CE and CCE selection (\citet{Borowski-learning15}, 18; \citet{Marden-selecting17}), single-bit satisfaction feedback extended to stochastic games, Kiremitci x3, logit-Q for stochastic teams \citep{Donmez-logitq25}, and team-optimal decentralised stochastic control \citep{Yongacoglu-decentralized22}.
\item G-NUM (\citet{Ramakrishnan-completely16}, 19) adds a K-slot memory. Thm 1: the stochastically stable states solve max\_p sum\_i U\_i(rbar\_i) with rbar\_i = sum\_a p(a) f\_i(a), U\_i possibly non-concave, over p on the grid \{0, 1/K..., 1\}. The mixing bound grows as eps\^{}\{-(c+1)NK\}.
\item Absorbed S7/learn/goore-game [\ensuremath{\approx}]: In Tsetlin's Goore game, N independent automata vote yes or no, and all receive the same stochastic reward, a unimodal function of the fraction voting yes.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Marden-achieving14}: promoted: seed of the team-optimum node; potential-free payoff-based selection of the welfare vertex
\item \citet{Ramakrishnan-completely16}: promoted: stochastic stability for utilities nonlinear in long-run averages, Thm 1; reduces to \citet{Marden-achieving14} at K=1 for our sign
\end{itemize}

\subsection{3. S7/heterogeneous}\label{node:S7-S7-heterogeneous}
\textbf{Relation} $\supset$: generalisation and precondition. Graphon and multi-class frameworks get uniqueness by contraction, small gain or a strongly convex own-action cost. Our own-occupancy objective is concave, so its best response is discontinuous, and it fails their core assumption \textbf{at the individual level, before any coupling}. Huang's freeze-mu, solve, refit-mu loop has the shape of our MM loop. Major-minor predicts leader-follower structure, not symmetric niches. For teams of finitely many symmetric subteams, cluster-exchangeable decentralised policies are optimal in the mean-field limit \citep{Braun-mean26}; against this team baseline our concave objective predicts symmetry breaking within a cluster.

\textbf{Idea.} Regulators that differ: graphon MFGs, where each agent's mean field is W-weighted; type-indexed, multi-class and multi-population MFGs; and major-minor MFGs, where a dominant regulator's state is common noise for all others. \citet{Huang-large06} has K classes and a sign-neutral cost whose worked example tracks the population mean; its uniqueness comes from the small-gain condition c1 c2 \textless{} 1. \citet{Carmona-kuramoto25} studies how random intrinsic frequencies change synchronisation.

\memb{Family S7.F4} graphon mean field games, 21 papers: {\footnotesize \citetext{\citealp{Caines-graphon20}; \citealp{Carmona-stochastic22}; \citealp{Cui-learning21}; \citealp{Caines-graphon20b}; \citealp{Gao-graphon20}; \citealp{Gao-lqg20}; \citealp{Vasal-sequential21}\dag; \citealp{Aurell-finite22}; \citealp{Aurell-stochastic22}; \citealp{Cui-hypergraphon22}; \citealp{Hu-graphon22}; \citealp{Lacker-label22}; \citealp{Fabian-learning22}; \citealp{Parise-graphon23}; \citealp{Zhang-learning23}; \citealp{Zhang-learning23b}; \citealp{Dong-last24}; \citealp{Rokade-graphon24}\dag; \citealp{Zhou-graphon24}; \citealp{Amini-extended26}; \citealp{Anand-graphon26}}}

\medskip\textsc{4. S7/collective: collectives minimising surprise or entropy}

\subsection{4.2 S7/collective/swarm-surprise}\label{node:S7-S7-collective-swarm-surprise}
\textit{Merged from} S7/collective/leup (5); S7/collective/aif-social (17). \textbf{Relation} $=$/$\subset$: same objective, team version. It is closest empirically. But the fitness is a shared team score, so it is a potential game, and controllers are identical, so any partition can only be positional. \citet{Kaiser-self19b} is the evolved, non-RL instance of 6.1: surprise lowered by shaping the disturbance. [S7/collective/leup] same objective, one-step special case. It is the only line found in which many agents each minimise an entropy of their own local outcome while forming each other's outcome. It differs from ours in three ways: the choice is a one-step Lagrangian rule, not a long-run outcome law; there is no learning; and the entropy is conditional. [S7/collective/aif-social] special case or opposite sign. Set-point and fixed-prior versions are our objective with the entropy weight set to 0. Hartwig's risk is cross-entropy \textbf{minus} outcome entropy, and influence and empowerment are maximised. So these supply the phenomena, not our objective. Yoshida 2025 finds prosociality only from summed drives, never from a joint outcome entropy. ELIGN is the one case where a predictability reward alone yields partition, with no explicit crowd-averse term; the mechanism is unverified at abstract level.

\textbf{Idea.} The Hamann/Kaiser "minimize surprise" line. Homogeneous swarms evolve actor-predictor pairs with fitness equal to the swarm-mean prediction accuracy. Density decides aggregation versus lines and lattices, and predefined predictions engineer patterns. Two variants matter for the root. \citet{Zahadat-evolving15b} adds a reward for each visited sensory state, an explicit anti-collapse term. \citet{Kaiser-self19b} robots push blocks until their world is predictable, lowering surprise by restructuring the environment.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Absorbed S7/collective/leup [=]: Least microenvironmental uncertainty principle.
\item Absorbed S7/collective/aif-social [\ensuremath{\subset}]: Active-inference collectives and social intrinsic rewards.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Kaiser-innate22}: closest empirical precedent: swarms evolved to minimise surprise, but with a shared team score and identical controllers
\item \citet{Barua-entropydriven20}: LEUP: cells each minimise entropy of their own microenvironment made of the others; narrows our gap-3 novelty claim outside RL
\item \citet{Heins-collective24}: collective motion from each agent minimising instantaneous free energy under a set-point; gives cohesion, not niches
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{5. S7/niches: when similar agents partition}

\subsection{5.1 S7/niches/coexistence}\label{node:S7-S7-niches-coexistence}
\textit{Merged from} S7/niches/specialisation (13). \textbf{Relation} \textsf{L}/$\approx$: precondition. Niches need a crowd-averse, self-limiting ingredient, or non-equilibrium density fluctuations; our MM loop need not settle, so the second route is open. Fretwell's Allee regime is our crowd-seeking regime seen in ecology. [S7/niches/specialisation] same mechanism, different objective, plus baselines. -H is Schur-convex, so \citet{Amir-when26} gives a checkable route to predicting niche partition. It is a hypothesis to prove. Threshold reinforcement drifts each agent to a vertex, as concave minimisation does.

\textbf{Idea.} The coexistence line: competitive exclusion, limiting similarity, R*, the ideal free distribution and adaptive-dynamics branching.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Coexistence needs self-limitation above cross-limitation, i.e. overlap rho \textless{} 1 in Chesson's stabilising term b\_i, 1 - rho, k\_s.
\item Competitive exclusion holds only at fixed densities, and fluctuating densities allow coexistence \citep{Armstrong-competitive80}.
\item There is no absolute limiting similarity, Abrams.
\item The IFD equalises suitability under crowd aversion, and Fretwell's Allee case gives non-unique, jumping distributions.
\item Novelty search with local competition keeps morphological diversity where global competition converges \citep{Lehman-evolving11}, the artificial-life form of self-limitation.
\item Absorbed S7/niches/specialisation [\ensuremath{\approx}]: Specialisation in MARL and in insect societies.
\end{itemize}

\medskip\textsc{6. S7/disturbance: regulators that engineer the disturbance}

\subsection{6.1 S7/disturbance/niche-construction}\label{node:S7-S7-disturbance-niche-construction}
\textit{Merged from} S7/disturbance/free-energy (3). \textbf{Relation} $\supset$/$=$: generalisation. A regulator can lower H(Z) by lowering the entropy of D instead of modelling it. The disturbance supplies the crowd-averse force. That reading is ours: Kylafis and Krakauer move equilibrium levels and carrying capacity and say nothing about fluctuations, so no paper here models variance or entropy reduction of D. [S7/disturbance/free-energy] same objective, conceptual or simulation only. The summed score is the analogue of -sum\_i H\_i. The refit rate of q against the environment's rate is our version of their precision ratio.

\textbf{Idea.} Organisms write a slow environmental variable that feeds back on selection, which gives momentum, hysteresis and polymorphism.
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item Construction changes turnover k, uptake f\_B or predation c\_i, and so moves equilibrium R and P and the coexistence cone, \citet{Kylafis-niche10} Eqs 2a-2d.
\item Stabilising constructors outsource memory into a low-entropy environment \citep{Lee-outsourcing22}.
\item Independent RL agents facing fire first herd, then engineer a chessboard firebreak.
\item Two subpopulations adapted to different resource values jointly regulate the resource by rein control, McDonald-Gibson08, Daisyworld lineage, now in S2.18.
\item NK-landscape coevolution through a shared constructed niche \citep{SuzukiArita-coevolution13}.
\item MARL shaping of others' learning and environments, LOLA, PAIRED, hide-and-seek shelter building in \citet{Baker-emergent20}, is kept here as a contrast. - \textbf{Note: the construction dilemma} [new-idea, recorded as a note, not a node]. In a shared niche w/w(0) = x/x(0) e\^{}\{(c'-c)t\}, so selection drives construction to zero; a monopoly probability m restores it at 1/(1+m), \citet{Krakauer-diversity09} Eqs 4-8. Lowering a shared disturbance is a public good, and own-entropy regulators face the same free-rider limit. It stays a note because the paper models a carrying-capacity level, not variance, and has one agent type.
\item Absorbed S7/disturbance/free-energy [=]: Agent and niche jointly minimise free energy.
\end{itemize}


\memb{Core.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=1.2em]
\item \citet{Bruineberg-free18}: nearest same-objective niche construction: agent and environment as symmetric count learners; relative rates select the attractor
\end{itemize}

\subsection{6.3 S7/disturbance/stigmergy}\label{node:S7-S7-disturbance-stigmergy}
\textbf{Relation} $\approx$: same mechanism. Our MM loop with a persistent pooled q is formally stigmergic. It predicts consensus on one niche unless the trace decays fast.

\textbf{Idea.} Coordination through traces in a shared medium: deposition plus following is reinforcement, and decay is forgetting. It gives clustering and sorting, a single trail, a least-time path, and diversity collapse under popularity feedback.
